i
Generative Models: Principles, Architectures, and
Applications
Jun Lu
jun.lu.locky@gmail.com


PREFACE
Generative AI has emerged as one of the most transformative forces in modern artificial
intelligence, reshaping how we create, imagine, and interact with digital content. From
photorealistic images to coherent text, from immersive videos to novel molecular struc-
tures, generative models now power applications that were once confined to science fiction.
This book is designed to guide readers through the foundational principles, mathematical
underpinnings, and practical architectures that underpin this revolution.
Our journey begins with the two cornerstone families of latent variable generative mod-
els: Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) and Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs). Here,
we unpack the Evidence Lower-Bound (ELBO), Expectation-Maximization (EM) algo-
rithms, adversarial training, and their many variants, providing both theoretical rigor and
practical insights into their strengths and limitations.
The core of the book is dedicated to Diffusion Models, the driving force behind today’s
state-of-the-art generative systems. We systematically cover both Diffusion Probabilistic
Models (DPMs) and Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs), walking readers
through the forward and reverse processes, the ELBO objective function, noise scheduling,
latent diffusion, and score-based interpretations. We also extend this discussion to guid-
ance mechanisms—including classifier and classifier-free guidance—and advanced sampling
techniques that enable high-fidelity, controllable generation.
From there, we turn to Flow-Based Models, another powerful class of generative frame-
works that offer exact likelihood computation and invertible transformations. We explore
neural differential equations, and score matching, drawing connections to diffusion mod-
els to highlight complementary strengths. Finally, we examine the practical architectures
that power modern image and video synthesis: U-Nets, ControlNet, Diffusion Transformers
(DiTs), and multimodal variants, alongside case studies of real-world systems like Stable
Diffusion 3.
No book of this nature is a solitary endeavor. We are deeply grateful to the countless
researchers who have paved the way for generative AI, whose pioneering work forms the
backbone of this text. It is our sincere hope that this book will serve as both an educational
resource and a source of inspiration as you push the boundaries of what generative models
can achieve.

This section provides a concise reference describing notation used throughout this book. If
you are unfamiliar with any of the corresponding mathematical concepts, the book describes
most of these ideas in Chapter 1 (p. 2).
Numbers and Arrays
a
A scalar (integer or real, italics font)
a
A vector (italics font)
A
A matrix (italics font)
ID
Identity matrix with D rows and D columns
I
Identity matrix with dimensionality implied by context
ei
Standard basis vector [0, . . . , 0, 1, 0, . . . , 0] with a 1 at position i
diag(a)
A square, diagonal matrix with diagonal entries given by a
a
A scalar random variable (normal fonts)
a
A vector-valued random variable (normal fonts)
A
A matrix-valued random variable (normal fonts)

CHAPTER 1.
INTRODUCTION
1.1. Introduction and Background
I
In just a decade, generative models have evolved from niche machine learning
curiosities into a cornerstone of modern artificial intelligence, reshaping how we
create, interact with, and interpret digital content. From photorealistic images
and immersive videos to coherent text and synthetic data, these models have demonstrated
an unprecedented ability to learn the underlying patterns of complex data distributions
and generate new, plausible samples that reflect the richness of the real world. This book
guides readers through the theoretical foundations, architectural innovations, and practical
advances that define this transformative field, providing a structured progression from core
principles to state-of-the-art systems.
▶Vector representations of images, videos, audio waveforms, molecular struc-
tures.
In this book, we focus primarily on the task of generating objects represented
as vectors x ∈RD, including images, videos, and molecular structures. In computational
modeling, complex data types such as images, videos, audio waveforms, and molecular struc-
tures are commonly encoded as high-dimensional vectors to enable mathematical analysis
and machine learning processing:
Images: An image is represented as a tensor x ∈RH×W×3 1, where H denotes
height, W denotes width, and the third dimension corresponds to the three RGB color
channels (Red, Green, Blue). This formulation captures the spatial and chromatic
information of the image in a structured numerical format.
Videos: A video is modeled as a temporal sequence of image frames, represented by
the tensor x ∈RT×H×W×3, where T is the number of frames. Each frame is treated
as an individual image, and concatenation along the time axis preserves the dynamic
evolution of the visual scene.
Audio waveforms: Raw audio signals are continuous one-dimensional time-series
data discretized for computational use. An audio waveform is represented as a ma-
trix x ∈RS×C, where S denotes the total number of discrete sampling points and
C denotes the number of audio channels (e.g., C = 1 for mono audio, C = 2 for
stereo audio). This structured numerical representation encodes temporal acoustic
variations, including amplitude, frequency, and sound duration, fully characterizing
the auditory features of the original audio signal.
Molecular structures: A molecular structure, such as a protein, is represented by
the coordinates of its constituent atoms. This is formulated as a matrix x ∈RN×3,
where N is the number of atoms, and each row specifies the 3D spatial coordinates
(x, y, z) of a given atom.
In all the above examples, the object we aim to generate can be mathematically represented
as a vector, typically after flattening. Accordingly, throughout this text, we treat the objects
being generated as vectors x ∈RD.
▶Different paradigms.
Broadly speaking, generative modeling research falls into two
fundamental paradigms.
These two frameworks differ drastically in their approaches to
1. Although this is a tensor, we still treat it as a vector x within the generative modeling framework.


4
1.1. INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND
factorizing data distributions and in the iterative mechanisms that drive the generative
process:
Token-based / sequential refinement (autoregressive, MaskGIT). These models oper-
ate within a discrete token space, typically produced by a learned tokenizer such as
VQVAE or VQGAN. They model the data distribution as a product of conditional
probability terms (Van Den Oord et al., 2017; Esser et al., 2021).
Latent-to-data mapping (GANs, VAEs, diffusion models, score-based models, and flow-
based models). These models learn a transformation that maps a simple prior dis-
tribution (most commonly a Gaussian distribution) to the target data distribution,
operating primarily in a continuous space.

Figure 1.1: The first paradigm of generative models: Sequential decoding and sched-
uled parallel decoding. In the corresponding visualization, rows 1 and 3 illustrate latent
mask inputs at each decoding iteration, while rows 2 and 4 present the generated samples
produced at each step. Traditional autoregressive models initialize generation with entirely
unknown latent codes (light gray) and fill in latent representations sequentially following a
fixed raster scan order (dark gray). MaskGIT decoding also starts with fully unknown la-
tent codes but adopts a parallel generation strategy: it populates latent representations via
progressively scattered token predictions (dark gray), with the number of predicted tokens
growing rapidly across iterations. Image source: Chang et al. (2022).
The first paradigm answers the core question: “Given partial data, what content comes
next?”. It factorizes the joint data distribution across individual data coordinates and per-
forms iterative generation within the discrete data or token space. Autoregressive (AR) mod-
els factorize the probability of data or token sequences x as p(x) = Q p(xi | xi−1, . . . , x1).
These models generate tokens sequentially, with each new token conditioned on all previ-
ously generated tokens. Classic examples include PixelRNN and PixelCNN (Van Den Oord
et al., 2016; Van den Oord et al., 2016), which explicitly model conditional probability distri-
butions. MaskGIT further extends this framework by relaxing the rigid left-to-right raster
ordering enforced by traditional autoregressive models (Chang et al., 2022). Trained with


5
CHAPTER 1.
INTRODUCTION
BERT-style masked token prediction, MaskGIT generates content by iteratively unmasking
and predicting the most confident tokens in parallel across a small number of decoding
steps. Across all token-based methods, iteration operates over discrete data dimensions or
tokens to determine which elements to generate next, and all outputs reside in a discrete
space. An intuitive illustration of this paradigm is provided in Figure 1.1.
Latent distribution: 
pbase(z)

pdata(x)

Figure 1.2: The second paradigm of generative models. Generative models in this
category convert samples drawn from a predefined latent prior distribution (e.g., Gaussian
distribution) into samples that conform to the real data distribution.
The second paradigm addresses a distinct core question: “How can we transform ran-
dom noise into realistic data?”.
It learns a deterministic or stochastic mapping from a
predefined prior distribution to the target data distribution, with iterative processes (where
applicable) operating in noise or continuous time space, rather than discrete token space.
Specifically, normalizing flows construct invertible transformations and compute exact data
likelihoods via the change-of-variables formula. GANs learn data mappings through adver-
sarial training without explicitly modeling likelihoods. Diffusion and score-based models
define a forward progressive noising process and learn the reverse denoising process, with
generation iterating over discrete noise levels (timesteps). Flow matching and rectified flow
models parameterize a continuous-time velocity field that transports prior distribution sam-
ples to realistic data samples via ordinary differential equations (ODEs). For all models in
this paradigm, iterative generation (when present) follows a continuous noise or transport
schedule, rather than iterating over individual data dimensions. The latent input is a full-
dimensional continuous noise vector, not a partial set of discrete tokens. See Figure 1.2 for
a conceptual demonstration.
While this book focuses primarily on the second paradigm—centered on transforming a
simple source distribution into the target distribution of real-world images—the boundary
between the two paradigms has become increasingly blurred in recent research. Modern
work frequently integrates core ideas from both frameworks. Hybrid approaches include
continuous autoregressive models, token-level diffusion models (e.g., discrete diffusion and
discrete flow models), and masked autoregressive (MAR) models.
These methods unify


6
1.1. INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND
autoregressive ordering rules with diffusion or flow-matching prediction heads for individual
tokens (Li et al., 2024; Team et al., 2025).
▶An alternative classification of generative models.
As discussed earlier, genera-
tive models encompass a diverse set of modeling strategies. A complementary classification
framework categorizes generative models based on their parameterization of the underly-
ing data distribution—specifically, whether they define the distribution pθ(x) explicitly or
implicitly, independent of their training objectives:
Explicit models. These models directly parameterize a probability distribution pθ(x)
via a tractable or approximately tractable density or mass function. Representative
examples include AR models, normalization flow models, VAEs, diffusion models, and
flow-matching models. All of these approaches define pθ(x) either exactly or through
a rigorously tractable lower/upper bound.
Implicit models. These models characterize the target distribution solely through a
stochastic sampling procedure, generally formulated as x = Gθ(z), where the latent
noise variable z is sampled from a predefined prior distribution pprior. Under this
formulation, pθ(x) does not have a closed-form expression and may not even be fully
analytically defined.
GANs serve as the canonical example of implicit generative
models.
▶Generation as sampling.
In mainstream generative modeling research, we typically
assume access to a finite training dataset consisting of independent and identically dis-
tributed (i.i.d.) samples drawn from an intractable, complex true data distribution pdata(x).
These observed data points constitute a finite dataset X = {x1, x2, . . . , xN} ∼pdata, which
acts as a practical surrogate for the inaccessible true distribution. The fundamental ob-
jective of generative modeling is to learn a tractable, approximate probability distribution
from this finite dataset, as the explicit analytical form of pdata(x) is unknown and direct
sampling from the true distribution is computationally infeasible.
To further elucidate the essence of the generative task, we formalize the definition of
“data generation” with intuitive examples. Take horse image generation as a typical case:
no single “optimal” horse image exists. Instead, there exists a continuous spectrum of plau-
sible horse images with varying degrees of visual realism and fidelity. In machine learning,
this diversity of valid outputs is rigorously modeled as a probability distribution over the
entire data space. Mathematically, pdata : RD →R+ denotes a nonnegative probability
density function that assigns a nonnegative likelihood score pdata(x) ≥0 to every feasi-
ble data sample x ∈RD. Samples that better conform to target semantic patterns (e.g.,
more realistic horse images) yield higher likelihood values under pdata. This formulation
transforms subjective qualitative evaluations of sample quality into objective quantitative
mathematical metrics, and strictly defines the generative task as sampling from the under-
lying unknown true data distribution pdata(x).
To approximate the intractable true data distribution, generative models leverage deep
neural networks to parameterize a learnable model distribution pθ(x), where θ denotes the
full set of trainable network parameters. The core training objective is to optimize these
parameters to obtain an optimal parameter set bθ, which minimizes the statistical divergence
between the learned model distribution pθ(x) and the true data distribution pdata(x). Ide-


7
CHAPTER 1.
INTRODUCTION
ally, the fully optimized model satisfies pbθ(x) ≈pdata(x).
After sufficient training, the
learned distribution pbθ(x) acts as a reliable surrogate for the true data distribution, en-
abling two core capabilities. First, sampling methods such as Monte Carlo sampling can be
deployed to generate unlimited novel, realistic data samples. Second, the likelihood pbθ(x′)
of any arbitrary sample x′ can be evaluated to quantify its plausibility under the learned
data distribution.
Beyond unconditional generation, numerous real-world applications demand conditional
generation guided by auxiliary contextual information, such as text prompts, categorical la-
bels, and other conditional variables y. For instance, the model can generate a customized
image conditioned on the text prompt: “A wild brown horse galloping down a snow-covered
grassy hillside, with rugged snow-capped mountain peaks towering across the horizon”. This
conditional generation task is mathematically formalized as sampling from the conditional
data distribution x ∼pdata(· | y), where pdata(· | y) represents the data distribution con-
strained by the conditioning variable y. This work primarily focuses on the theoretical
framework of unconditional generative models, with extensions to conditional generative
models elaborated in Sections 3.4, 5.3, and Chapter 7.
Focusing on the second research paradigm visualized in Figure 1.2, all mainstream gen-
erative models can be uniformly framed as a distribution transformation problem.
This
unified framework first defines a simple, tractable base prior distribution pbase, conven-
tionally adopted as the standard multivariate Gaussian distribution pbase = N(0, IK) with
K ≤D. A generative model is essentially a learned nonlinear transformation that maps
simple prior noise samples to complex real-world data samples. The standard generative
process is formalized as follows:
z ∼pbase
Generative Model
−−−−−−−−−−−→x ∼pdata.
This high-level paradigm unifies all dominant generative modeling architectures—including
generative adversarial networks (GANs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), normalizing
flows, diffusion models, and flow-matching models—under a universal perspective: all these
models learn a deterministic or stochastic transformation that converts a simple fixed prior
distribution into a complex target data distribution.
▶Similarity between the two paradigms.
Despite adopting distinct approaches to
content generation, diffusion/flow-matching models and autoregressive (AR) models share
several fundamental similarities in their data generation mechanisms, even though their
specific implementations and inference pipelines differ substantially.
The most essential
commonality is that neither paradigm produces complete data outputs in a single forward
pass. Instead of one-shot generation, both rely on a step-by-step incremental construction
strategy:
AR models: Generate data sequentially by predicting one token or pixel at a time,
constructing the full output in a fixed order (e.g., top-to-bottom or left-to-right for
visual data).
Diffusion/flow-matching models: Produce high-fidelity data through iterative denois-
ing. They gradually refine randomly initialized noisy inputs across numerous steps,
transforming pure Gaussian noise into coherent, realistic target data such as clear
images.


8
1.1. INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND
Furthermore, autoregressive models, VAEs, and diffusion models are all fundamentally
likelihood-based generative models. Their training objectives uniformly aim to maximize the
log-likelihood of training data under their respective generative frameworks, with paradigm-
specific optimization strategies:
AR models: Directly maximize the conditional probability of each individual token
given its preceding contextual sequence.
VAE and diffusion models: Optimize data likelihood indirectly by minimizing the
evidence lower-bound (ELBO). This optimization objective can be further decomposed
into a sequence of simple, tractable denoising sub-objectives for iterative training.
▶Book structure.
To conclude, generative AI marks a pivotal paradigm shift in ma-
chine learning, shifting the field’s focus from conventional predictive tasks such as classi-
fication and regression to creative generative tasks centered on novel data synthesis. This
transformative advancement is enabled by major breakthroughs in latent variable model-
ing, diffusion processes, flow-based optimization methods, and large-scale neural network
architectures. These advances have culminated in state-of-the-art systems including Stable
Diffusion, DALL-E, and Meta Movie Gen, which significantly bridge the gap between hu-
man creativity and machine-generated content. Behind these widely recognized practical
applications lies a rigorous mathematical framework built on probability theory, variational
inference, and deep learning—the core theoretical foundations that this book systematically
elaborates and explains in depth.
The book is structured to help readers build professional expertise in generative mod-
eling progressively, starting from basic foundational concepts and gradually advancing to
sophisticated modern architectures, with the organizational breakdown as follows:
Part 1: Foundations (Chapters 1–2) establishes the fundamental mathematical and
theoretical framework of generative modeling. This part covers essential mathemat-
ical notations, core probability theories, variational inference principles, and classic
latent variable models including VAEs and GANs. It demystifies key concepts such as
the evidence lower-bound (ELBO), adversarial training mechanisms, and amortized
inference, equipping readers with solid theoretical knowledge and practical tools to
analyze and implement basic generative models.
Part 2: Diffusion models (Chapter 3) focuses on the dominant technical paradigm
in contemporary generative AI. It comprehensively introduces diffusion probabilistic
models (DPMs), denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs), latent diffusion
models (LDMs), and conditional guidance mechanisms. This chapter elaborates on
how diffusion models iteratively purify random noise into structured, coherent data,
empowering high-quality generation of images, videos, and other complex data modal-
ities.
Part 3: Flow-based and score-based models (Chapters 4–5) explores alternative main-
stream generative modeling frameworks, encompassing standard normalizing flows,
continuous flow models, and score matching methods. Distinct from diffusion models,
these architectures support exact likelihood calculation and deliver stable training per-
formance, serving as effective complementary approaches to diffusion-based generative
systems.


9
CHAPTER 1.
INTRODUCTION
Part 4: Advanced sampling methods (Chapter 6) delve into the practical acceleration
and refinement of diffusion model sampling, addressing the computational bottleneck
of traditional stepwise generation. Building on the theoretical SDE/ODE foundations
of diffusion processes, it introduces state-of-the-art sampling algorithms—including
DEIS, DPM-Solver, and DPM-Solver++—that drastically reduce the number of re-
quired sampling steps while preserving output fidelity. By bridging continuous-time
stochastic dynamics with efficient numerical solvers, this part equips readers with the
tools to deploy high-speed, high-quality generative systems for real-world applications,
from image synthesis to video generation.
Part 5: Modern generative architectures (Chapter 7) discusses the key architectural
and engineering innovations that underpin modern large-scale generative AI systems.
It covers core modules and technologies including CLIP, attention mechanisms, U-
Nets, ControlNet, diffusion transformers (DiTs), and multimodal generative frame-
works.
This part also explores latent space manipulation strategies and presents
in-depth case studies of cutting-edge models such as Stable Diffusion 3.
1.2. Notations and Mathematical Tools
In the remainder of this chapter, we introduce and review some fundamental concepts from
linear algebra and probability theory that are relevant to generative models. Additional
important concepts will be defined and discussed as needed throughout the text for clarity.
In all cases, scalars will be denoted in non-bold font, possibly with subscripts (e.g.,
a, α, αi). Vectors will be represented by boldface lowercase letters, again possibly with
subscripts (e.g., µ, x, xn, z), and matrices by boldface uppercase letters, also possibly
with subscripts (e.g., X, Lj). However, we will use x to denote the data object in generative
modeling notation, even when it represents a matrix; see Chapter 7. The i-th element of a
vector z will be written as zi in non-bold font. To distinguish deterministic quantities from
random ones, we adopt the following convention:
Random variables (scalars) are denoted using upright (non-italic) font (e.g., a and b1
are random variables, whereas italicized a and b1 denote deterministic scalars).
Random vectors are indicated by upright bold lowercase letters, possibly with sub-
scripts (e.g., a and b1 are random vectors, while italicized bold a and b1 represent
deterministic vectors).
Random matrices are denoted by upright bold uppercase letters, possibly with sub-
scripts (e.g., A and B1 are random matrices, whereas italicized bold A and B1 denote
deterministic matrices).
Subarrays are formed by fixing a subset of indices of a matrix. The element located in
the i-th row and j-th column of a matrix X (i.e., the (i, j) entry) is denoted by xij.
Consequently, a matrix X ∈RN×D may be expressed as X = {xij}N,D
i,j=1 = [xij].
For
notational convenience, we adopt Matlab-style indexing:
The submatrix consisting of rows i through j and columns k through m of X is
denoted by Xi:j,k:m ≡X[i : j, k : m].
A colon is used to indicate all elements along a dimension. For example, X:,k:m ≡X[:
, k : m] denotes the submatrix formed by columns k through m; and X:,k ≡X[:, k]
denotes the k-th column of X.


10
1.2. NOTATIONS AND MATHEMATICAL TOOLS
Alternatively, the k-th column of X may be denoted more compactly by xk; and the
k-th row of X can be denoted as x(k).
Throughout this book, all vectors are assumed to be column vectors unless explicitly
transposed. A row vector is denoted as the transpose of a column vector, e.g., x⊤. Concrete
vectors are written using MATLAB-style syntax. A column vector is written with semicolons
“; ” separating entries, e.g.,
x = [1; 2; 3]
(column vector)
is a column vector in R3. Similarly, A row vector uses commas “, ” to separate entries, e.g.,
y = [1, 2, 3]
(row vector)
is a row vector with three entries. Equivalently, a column vector may be expressed as the
transpose of a row vector: y = [1, 2, 3]⊤is a column vector.
The transpose of a matrix X is denoted by X⊤, and its inverse (when it exists) by
X−1. The D × D identity matrix is denoted by ID or simply by I. A vector or matrix of
all zeros is denoted by the boldface symbol 0; its dimensions are inferred from context, or
explicitly indicated as 0D for a D-dimensional zero vector. Similarly, a vector or matrix of
all ones is denoted by a boldface symbol 1, whose dimensions are clear from context; or
we write 1D to denote the vector of all ones with D entries. Subscripts are often omitted
when the dimensions are evident from context.
1.2.1 Norms
The concept of a norm is fundamental to quantifying the magnitude of vectors or and
enables the formal definition of metrics on normed linear spaces. Norms provide a rigorous
numerical measure of the “size” of vectors and matrices, serving as a foundational tool
across numerous applications. Typical use cases include calculating the Euclidean length of
a vector and quantifying the scale of matrices in high-dimensional multivariate analysis.
Furthermore, norms facilitate the definition of distances between vectors and matrices.
Specifically, the distance between two vectors u and v is defined as the norm of their dif-
ference, namely ∥u −v∥. This distance formulation underpins proximity-based algorithms,
including common clustering methods in machine learning. 2
Any valid vector or matrix norm must satisfy three core axioms, as formally stated
below (see, for example, Lu (2021a) for comprehensive background).
Definition 1.1 (Vector norm and matrix nrom).
Let ∥·∥denote a generic norm
function for vectors and matrices. For any matrix X ∈RN×D and any vector β ∈RD,
the following three properties hold:
Nonnegativity. ∥X∥≥0 and ∥β∥≥0. Equality holds if and only if X = 0 and
β = 0, respectively.
Positive homogeneity. ∥λX∥= |λ| · ∥X∥and ∥λβ∥= |λ| · ∥β∥for any scale λ ∈R.
2. Our discussion focuses exclusively on norms and inner products defined over real vector and matrix
spaces. All corresponding results can be directly extended to the complex domain.

Triangle inequality. For all matrices X, Y ∈RN×D, ∥X + Y ∥≤∥X∥+ ∥Y ∥. For
all vectors α, β ∈RD, ∥α + β∥≤∥α∥+ ∥β∥.
Based on the general norm axioms above, we introduce several commonly used vector
norms, including the ℓ1, ℓ2, and ℓ∞-norms, as well as the generalized ℓp-norm.

Beyond the operator-based matrix norms introduced above, an alternative approach to
defining matrix norms is to vectorize a matrix X ∈RN×D and treat it as a standard vector in
RND. What fundamentally distinguishes rigorous matrix norms from naive vector-induced
matrix magnitudes is a critical property known as submultiplicativity: for any submultiplica-
tive matrix norm ∥·∥, the inequality ∥XY ∥≤∥X∥∥Y ∥holds for all compatible matrices
X and Y . See, for example, Lu (2021a) for more details.
1.2.2 Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)
This subsection introduces the matrix singular value decomposition (SVD). Before present-
ing the general SVD formulation, we first review the spectral decomposition of symmetric
matrices, which serves as a foundational special case of SVD. The spectral theorem (or
spectral decomposition) for symmetric matrices states that every real symmetric matrix
possesses real eigenvalues and is fully diagonalizable over an orthonormal real basis. 3
Theorem 1.5: (Spectral decomposition)
A real square matrix X ∈RD×D is sym-
metric if and only if there exist an orthogonal matrix Q and a diagonal matrix Λ such
that
X = QΛQ⊤,
where the columns of Q = [q1, q2, . . . , qD] form a set of mutually orthonormal eigenvectors
of X, and the diagonal entries of Λ = diag(λ1, λ2, . . . , λD) are the corresponding real
eigenvalues. In particular, symmetric matrices satisfy the following key properties:
1. All eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix are real-valued.
2. Its eigenvectors can be selected to form an orthonormal set.
3. The rank of X equals the number of its nonzero eigenvalues.
4. If all eigenvalues are distinct, then the corresponding eigenvectors are linearly inde-
pendent.
Proof See Lu (2021a).
3. The spectral decomposition for Hermitian matrices yields an analogous result: Hermitian matrices have
real eigenvalues and admit diagonalization with respect to a complex orthonormal basis.


13
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Spectral decomposition provides a clean diagonal factorization for symmetric square
matrices. However, this framework is limited: diagonalization via eigen decomposition is
generally unavailable for non-symmetric or non-square matrices. Singular value decompo-
sition (SVD) resolves this limitation by generalizing the decomposition to any real matrix.
Instead of a single orthogonal eigenvector matrix, SVD factorizes an arbitrary matrix into
the product of two orthogonal matrices and a diagonal matrix of singular values. We now
formally state the SVD theorem.
Theorem 1.6: (Full singular value decomposition)
Every real N × D matrix X
with rank r admits a decomposition of the form
X = UΣV ⊤,
where Σ ∈RN×D follows the block structure Σ =

Σr
0
0
0

with Σr = diag(σ1, σ2 . . . , σr) ∈
Rr×r. The singular values are sorted in non-increasing order: σ1 ≥σ2 ≥. . . ≥σr.
The scalars σi denote the nonzero singular values of X.
Each singular value σi
equals the positive square root of a nonzero eigenvalue shared by both X⊤X and
XX⊤.
U ∈RN×N is an orthogonal matrix. Its first r columns correspond to the eigen-
vectors of XX⊤associated with the r nonzero eigenvalues, while the remaining
N −r columns form an orthonormal basis for the null space of X⊤: N(X⊤) ≜{α |
X⊤α = 0}.
V ∈RD×D is an orthogonal matrix. Its first r columns correspond to the eigenvectors
of X⊤X associated with the r nonzero eigenvalues, while the remaining D −r
columns form an orthonormal basis for the null space of X: N(X) ≜{β | Xβ = 0}.
The columns of U and V are referred to as the left and right singular vectors of X,
respectively.
Furthermore, the full SVD can be rewritten as a summation of rank-one outer-product
matrices: X = UΣV ⊤= Pr

i .
For a rank-r matrix X, the first r singular values satisfy σ1, σ2, . . . , σr > 0, while all
subsequent singular values are identically zero: σr+1 = σr+2 = . . . = 0. In most practical
applications, the truncated reduced singular value decomposition (reduced SVD) is preferred
for its compact form.
Given the full SVD X = UΣV ⊤of a rank-r matrix X, we define the truncated sub-
matrices Ur ∈RN×r and Vr ∈RD×r by retaining only the first r columns of U and V ,
respectively: U = [Ur, ur+1, . . . , uN] and V = [Vr, vr+1, . . . , vD]. We also retain the pos-
itive rank-r singular value matrix Σr = diag(σ1, . . . , σr) ∈Rr×r. This yields the reduced
SVD factorization:
X = UrΣrV ⊤

1.2.3 Probability Theory
A random variable is a quantity that takes different values governed by inherent random-
ness, and it is widely used to model uncertain outcomes and stochastic events.
In this
book, we adopt a consistent typographic convention to distinguish random quantities from
their observed realizations: random variables are denoted by lowercase letters in upright
(non-italic) font, while their realized or observed values are denoted by lowercase italic
letters. For example, y1 and y2 are valid realizations of the random variable y.
We extend this convention to vector-valued and matrix-valued random quantities. Ran-
dom vectors (RVs) are written in upright bold font (e.g., y), and their specific realizations
are written in italic bold font (e.g., y). Similarly, random matrices (RMs) are denoted by
upright bold capital letters (e.g., Y), whereas their corresponding deterministic realizations


15
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
adopt italic bold capital letters (e.g., Y ). This strict typographic distinction eliminates
ambiguity between stochastic quantities and their observed values throughout the text.
A standalone random variable only characterizes the set of all possible outcomes of a
stochastic process. To fully quantify stochastic behavior, a random variable must be paired
with a probability distribution that quantifies the likelihood of each possible outcome. In
probability theory, the outcome of a random process is modeled as a random variable, whose
overall statistical behavior is fully described by its cumulative distribution function (CDF),
defined as
F(x) ≡Pr[x ≤x].
(1.6)
In practice, the probabilistic behavior of a random variable x is often controlled by an
underlying characteristic θ, referred to as a model parameter. Since the probability of the
event {x ≤x} depends explicitly on θ, the corresponding CDF is parameterized accordingly:
F(x; θ) ≡Prθ[x ≤x].
(1.7)
When both the functional form F(x; θ) and the true parameter θ are known, the probability
of any outcome x ≤x can be directly evaluated via the parameterized CDF.
▶Discrete random variables.
Random variables fall into two primary categories: dis-
crete and continuous. A discrete random variable takes values over a finite or countably
infinite set of distinct outcomes. These outcomes are not required to be numerical and
can instead represent categorical or symbolic states, such as “red,” “blue,” “success,” or
“failure”. In contrast, a continuous random variable takes values over an uncountable set,
typically a continuous subset of the real line.
Probability distributions assign probabilistic weights to the outcomes of random vari-
ables, and their mathematical representation differs substantially for discrete and continuous
variables.
For discrete random variables, we adopt the probability mass function (PMF), commonly
denoted as Pr or f. The PMF maps each feasible outcome of a discrete random variable to
its corresponding probability. We use several equivalent notations for the probability that
the random variable y takes the specific value y:
Pr(y = y)
(or equivalently Pr(y), fy(y), Pry(y)).
(1.8)
By definition, all probabilities lie within the interval [0, 1], where 1 denotes a certain event
and 0 denotes an impossible event. A standard shorthand for specifying the distribution of
a random variable uses the distribution symbol “∼”, written as
y ∼Pr(y).
(1.9)
PMFs can be generalized to characterize joint probabilistic behavior across multiple
random variables, known as the joint probability mass function (joint PMF). For two discrete
random variables x and y, the joint PMF Pr(x = x, y = y) quantifies the probability that
the outcomes x and y occur simultaneously. We frequently use the simplified notations
Pr(x, y) or Prx,y(x, y). When the joint distribution depends on a parameter set θ, we write
Pr(x, y | θ) (or Prθ(x, y), f(x, y; θ)) for compactness.


16
1.2. NOTATIONS AND MATHEMATICAL TOOLS
In practical probabilistic modeling, we often evaluate the likelihood of one event con-
ditioned on the occurrence of another, which is formalized as conditional probability. The
conditional probability of observing x = x given that y = y is defined as
Pr(x = x | y = y) = Pr(x = x, y = y)
Pr(y = y)
,
(1.10)
which is valid whenever Pr(y = y) > 0. This fundamental identity serves as the foundation
for Bayes’ theorem (Bayes, 1958).
Given a joint distribution over a collection of random variables, we often derive the
marginal distribution that describes the statistical behavior of a single subset of variables by
marginalizing out the remaining variables. For discrete settings, the marginal distribution
of x is obtained by summing the joint PMF over all feasible outcomes of y:
Pr(x = x) =
X
y
Pr(x = x, y = y).
(1.11)
▶Continuous random variables.
For continuous random variables, probability dis-
tributions are characterized by a probability density function (PDF), which replaces the
PMF used for discrete variables. A valid PDF p must satisfy the following three axiomatic
properties:
The domain of p covers all possible values of the random variable y.
Unlike a PMF, a PDF value is not bounded above by one, i.e., p(y) may exceed 1.
However, the PDF must be everywhere nonnegative: p(y) ≥0 for all valid outcomes
y of y.
The PDF integrates to unity over its entire domain:
R
p(y) dy = 1. Throughout this
book, we omit integration limits when the integral is taken over the full real space,
such that
R
≡
R
R.
Crucially, the PDF value p(y) (also written fy(y) or py(y) 4) does not represent a probability
itself; it represents a probability density. The probability of the random variable falling
within an infinitesimal interval of width δy centered at y is approximately p(y)δy. More
formally, for any measurable set A, the probability that the realized value y belongs to A
is computed by integrating the PDF over that set:
Pr(y ∈A) =
Z
A
p(y) dy,
where
Z
p(y) dy = 1.
(1.12)
When the PDF is parameterized by a parameter vector θ, we adopt several standard and
interchangeable notations:
px(x | θ),
p(x | θ),
pθ(x),
fx(x; θ),
or simply f(x; θ).
The vertical bar or semicolon denotes dependence on the fixed model parameters θ.

of a random vector xt as pt.

Distribution Transformation
We now present key results for the expectation and covariance of linearly and quadratically
transformed random vectors.
Lemma 1.9: (Linear transformation)
Let x ∈RD be a random vector with mean
µ ∈RD and covariance matrix Ω∈RD×D. Define the affine transformation y = Ax + b,
where A ∈RM×D and b ∈RM are deterministic constant matrix and vector, respectively.
The transformed mean and covariance satisfy
E[y] = Aµ + b
and
Cov[y] = AΩA⊤.
Proof [of Lemma 1.9] The formula for the mean follows directly from the linearity of
expectation. For the covariance, we substitute the definition of the covariance matrix:
Cov[y] = Cov[Ax] = E[A(x −µ)(x −µ)⊤A⊤] = AE[(x −µ)(x −µ)⊤]A⊤= AΩA⊤.
This completes the proof.
As a special case, let A = a⊤be a row vector, so that y = a⊤x defines a linear functional
of x. If x has uncorrelated entries with identical variance, i.e., Cov[x] = σ2I, then the
variance of the transformed scalar random variable simplifies to Cov[y] = σ2a⊤a.
Lemma 1.10: (Quadratic transformation)
Let A ∈RD×D be a symmetric matrix,
and let x ∈RD be a random vector with mean µ = [µi] ∈RD and covariance Ω= [ωij] ∈
RD×D. Then
E[x⊤Ax] = µ⊤Aµ + tr(AΩ),
where tr(AΩ) denotes the trace of AΩ, i.e., the sum of its diagonal entries.
Proof [of Lemma 1.10] Since x⊤Ax = PD

In many practical settings, random vectors are centered such that µ = 0. Under zero mean,
the quadratic expectation simplifies to E[x⊤Ax] = tr(AΩ). If, further, Ω= σ2Σ, then
E[x⊤Ax] = σ2tr(AΣ). To obtain an unbiased estimator of σ2, we can choose a matrix A
such that tr(AΣ) = 1 (Lu, 2021b).

1.2.4 Gaussian Distribution and Properties
The Gaussian (or normal) distribution is one of the most widely adopted statistical models
in data modeling, machine learning, and signal processing. Its popularity stems from a
fundamental probabilistic result: the sum of many independent random variables asymp-
totically follows a Gaussian distribution, a property rigorously established by the central
limit theorem.
5 Consequently, any measurement or macroscopic quantity composed of
numerous independent additive microscopic components tends to be approximately Gaus-
sian. For this reason, observational and sensor noise in engineering and signal processing
is routinely modeled as Gaussian noise, owing to its favorable analytical properties and
mathematical tractability.
The multivariate Gaussian distribution (also referred to as the multivariate normal
(MVN) distribution) generalizes the univariate Gaussian to vector-valued random variables
and characterizes the joint distribution of a set of normally distributed random components.
A multivariate Gaussian distribution is fully specified by two finite-dimensional parameters:
a mean vector and a positive-definite covariance matrix. The covariance matrix encodes the
statistical behavior of the random vector, including individual component variances along
its diagonal entries and pairwise linear correlation among components via its off-diagonal
entries. Due to its analytical simplicity, closed-form algebraic properties, and wide empirical
applicability, the multivariate Gaussian serves as a foundational building block throughout
statistics, machine learning, and signal processing. We present its formal definition below.
5. See, for example, Lu (2026).


22
1.2. NOTATIONS AND MATHEMATICAL TOOLS
Definition 1.11 (Multivariate Gaussian distribution).
A random vector x ∈RD
is said to follow the multivariate Gaussian distribution (multivariate normal, MVN) with
parameters µ ∈RD and Σ ∈RD×D, denoted x ∼N(µ, Σ), if
p(x; µ, Σ) = (2π)−D/2 |Σ|−1/2 exp

where µ ∈RD is called the mean vector, and Σ ∈RD×D is positive definite and is called
the covariance matrix. |Σ| = det(Σ) denotes the determinant of Σ. The mean, mode,
and covariance of the multivariate Gaussian distribution are given by
E[x] = µ,
Mode[x] = µ,
and
Cov[x] = Σ.
The covariance matrix can also be expressed as:
Cov[x] = E[(x −µ)(x −µ)⊤] = E[xx⊤] −µµ⊤.
a. The form of which can be proved using the moment generating function of D i.i.d. univariate standard
Gaussian variables.
Figure 1.5 visualizes multivariate Gaussian density contours under different covariance
structures. A zero-mean Gaussian random vector with identity covariance matrix consists
of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) standard Gaussian components with zero
mean and unit variance. More generally, arbitrary multivariate Gaussian vectors can be
constructed via linear transformation of univariate Gaussian samples; see Problem 1.2 for
detailed construction.
Properties of Multivariate Gaussian Distribution
A defining property of Gaussian random vectors is that affine transformations preserve
Gaussianity. This invariance property can be derived either via the linear transformation
rule for probability distributions (Lemma 1.9) or using the moment generating function of
the multivariate Gaussian distribution (Lu, 2026).
Lemma 1.12: (Affine transformation of multivariate Gaussian distribution)
Let x ∼N(µx, Σx) and y ∼N(µy, Σy) be independent D-dimensional Gaussian ran-
dom vectors. For deterministic matrices A, B ∈RN×D and deterministic vector c ∈RN,
the affine combination follows a multivariate Gaussian distribution:
z = Ax + By + c ∼N(Aµx + Bµy + c, AΣxA⊤+ BΣyB⊤).
Furthermore, for any fixed vector d ∈RD, the linear combination d⊤x yields a univariate
Gaussian random variable:
d⊤x ∼N(d⊤µx, d⊤Σxd).
This result also generalizes to the sum of independent Gaussian vectors. For a collection
of independent Gaussian vectors satisfying xi ∼N(µi, Σi) for all i ∈{1, 2, . . . , N}, their

Two critical implications follow directly from Lemma 1.12. First, any subvector of a
multivariate Gaussian random vector is itself Gaussian. Second, an i.i.d. standard Gaussian


24
1.2. NOTATIONS AND MATHEMATICAL TOOLS
vector is isotropic, meaning its probability distribution is invariant under arbitrary orthog-
onal transformations. Formally, consider an i.i.d. standard Gaussian vector x. For any
orthogonal matrix Q (satisfying Q⊤Q = QQ⊤= I), the transformed vector Qx preserves
the original distribution. This holds because the transformed mean is Q0 = 0 and the trans-
formed covariance matrix is QIQ⊤= I. Importantly, isotropy is a stronger property than
identical marginal variances: it ensures the entire distribution (not merely second-order
moments) remains unchanged under rotation.
Lemma 1.13: (Rotations on multivariate Gaussian distribution)
Multivariate
Gaussian distributions with spherical covariance matrices are rotationally invariant. Specif-
ically, for any orthogonal matrix Q, if x ∼N(0, σ2I), then the rotated vector satisfies
Qx ∼N(0, σ2I).
▶“Standardization and decorrelation.”
A random vector following the zero-mean
identity-covariance Gaussian distribution N(0, I) is termed an i.i.d. standard Gaussian
vector. Arbitrary multivariate Gaussian vectors can be standardized and decorrelated via
covariance whitening. For any x ∼N(µ, Σ), the standardized vector follows the standard
Gaussian distribution:
x ∼N(µ, Σ)
=⇒
z = Σ−1/2(x −µ) ∼N(0, I).
(1.27)
Conversely, any general multivariate Gaussian vector can be reconstructed from a standard
Gaussian vector via scaling and shifting: if x ∼N(µ, Σ), then
x = µ + Σ1/2ϵ,
where ϵ ∼N(0, I).
(1.28)

from N(µ, Σ), and define the sample mean x =

Lemma 1.14: (Uncorrelation implies mutual independence for Gaussian ran-
dom vectors)
For a Gaussian random vector, pairwise uncorrelation among all compo-
nents guarantees their mutual independence.
This property is unique to Gaussian distributions and follows directly from the structure of
the multivariate Gaussian PDF. When the covariance matrix is diagonal, all cross-covariance
terms vanish, and the joint probability density function factorizes exactly into the product
of univariate marginal densities, which is the defining condition for mutual independence.
We now formalize this result for partitioned Gaussian vectors. Let x ∼N(µ, Σ) with
µ ∈RD, and partition the random vector and its parameters into two subvectors and
corresponding submatrices:

the xi’s are mutually independent if and only if Σ is diagonal.

joint PDF decomposes into a product of individual marginal PDFs, which implies mutual
independence of all scalar components.
This fundamental property yields the following useful corollary concerning linear trans-
formations of Gaussian vectors.
Corollary 1.15: (Independence of linear combinations in Gaussian Distribu-
tions)
Let x ∼N(µ, Σ) be a random vector in RD , and let A ∈RM×D, B ∈RN×D be
fixed matrices. Then,
Ax is independent of Bx
⇐⇒
AΣB⊤= 0.
(1.31)
This result can also be proven via moment generating function properties of multivariate
Gaussian distributions; we omit the detailed derivation here.
In addition, standard probabilistic invariance guarantees that measurable functions of
independent random vectors remain independent. Specifically, if x1 and x2 are independent,
then G1(x1) and G2(x2) are independent for any pair of measurable functions G1, G2.
1.2.5 Differentiable Functions and Differential Calculus
Differentiability and differential calculus form the foundational framework of mathematical
analysis for multi-dimensional functions.
This subsection introduces core differentiation
concepts that characterize how multivariate functions change with their inputs, providing
rigorous tools for function analysis, optimization, and system modeling.
Central to multivariate calculus is the notion of the directional derivative, which quanti-
fies the instantaneous rate of change of a function G at a point x along a specified direction
vector d.


26
1.2. NOTATIONS AND MATHEMATICAL TOOLS

notations include ∂G

derivatives at that point.

Exercise 1.17.
Prove the following two counterintuitive properties of multivariate
functions:
1. A function may possess well-defined partial derivatives at a point yet fail to be
continuous at that point. a
2. A function may be continuous at a point yet lack well-defined partial derivatives
at that point.
a. A function G(x) is continuous at a if limx→a G(x) = G(a); a function is continuous overall if it is
continuous at every point in its domain.
A function G defined on an open set S ⊆RD is called differentiable if all its partial
derivatives exist (i.e., the derivatives exist in univariate cases, and the gradients exist in
multivariate cases). This is actually the definition of Fr´echet differentiability.
Definition 1.18 ((Fr´echet) differentiability).
Let f : RD →R. The function G is
(Fr´echet) differentiable at x ∈RD if there exists a vector g ∈RD such that
lim
d→0
G(x + d) −G(x) −g⊤d
∥d∥2
= 0.
The vector g is unique and coincides with the gradient ∇G(x).
A function G defined on an open set S ⊆RD is continuously differentiable on S if all of
its first-order partial derivatives exist and are continuous throughout S.
Importantly, differentiability does not imply continuous differentiability: a function can
be differentiable everywhere while possessing discontinuous partial derivatives. A classic
one-dimensional counterexample demonstrates this distinction. Define
G(x) =
(

if x = 0.
This function is differentiable over the entire real line. For all x̸ = 0, G(x) is a product
of two differentiable functions and is therefore differentiable. At x = 0, the derivative is
verified via the limit definition:

The limit evaluates to zero because |sin(1/µ)| ≤1 for all µ̸ = 0, yielding a bounded oscil-
lation that vanishes as µ →0. Thus, G′(0) = 0. Nevertheless, G(x) is not continuously
differentiable at x = 0. For x̸ = 0, direct differentiation gives
G′(x) = 2x sin(1

x).
As x →0, the term cos(1/x) oscillates indefinitely without convergence, so limx→0 G′(x)
does not exist. The first derivative is therefore discontinuous at the origin.

For high-dimensional functions, we have the following approximation results.
Theorem 1.19: (Mean value theorem)
Let f : S →R be a continuously differentiable
function over an open set S ⊆RD, and given two points x, y ∈S. Then, there exists a
point ξ ∈[x, y] such that
G(y) = G(x) + ∇G(ξ)⊤(y −x).
Proof [of Theorem 1.19] Without loss of generality, we assume ξ(t) = x + t(y −x) with
t ∈[0, 1]. Then we define g(t) ≜G(ξ(t)). We can apply the ordinary mean value theorem
to g(t), to get
g(1) = g(0) + g′(α),
for some α in the interval [0, 1]. The derivative of g(t) is
g′(t) = ⟨∇G(ξ(t)), y −x⟩,
where
∇G(ξ(t)) =
 ∂G
∂ξ1
(ξ(t)), ∂G
∂ξ2
(ξ(t)), . . . , ∂G
∂ξD
(ξ(t))
⊤
.
Therefore, g′(α) = ⟨∇G(ξ(α)), y −x⟩. Since ξ(α) = (1 −α)x + αy, this completes the
proof.
We provide linear approximation and quadratic approximation theorems without a
proof.
Theorem 1.20: (Linear approximation theorem)
Let f : S →R be a twice contin-
uously differentiable function on an open set S ⊆RD, and let x, y ∈S. Then, there exists
a point ξ ∈[x, y] such that
G(y) = G(x) + ∇G(x)⊤(y −x) + 1
2(y −x)⊤∇2G(ξ)(y −x),
or
G(y) = G(x) + ∇G(x)⊤(y −x) + o(∥y −x∥2),


30
Chapter 1 Problems
or
G(y) = G(x) + ∇G(x)⊤(y −x) + O(∥y −x∥2
2).
This theorem, also known as the first-order Taylor series expansion, suggests that the error
in the linear approximation is of the order of the square of the distance between x and y.
And the first-order Taylor series expansion G(y) ≈G(x) + ∇G(x)⊤(y −x) is exact only
for the neighborhood of y at the point x.
Theorem 1.21: (Quadratic approximation theorem)
Let f : S →R be a twice
continuously differentiable function on an open set S ⊆RD, and let x, y ∈S. Then it
follows that
G(y) = G(x) + ∇G(x)⊤(y −x) + 1
2(y −x)⊤∇2G(x)(y −x) + o(∥y −x∥2
2),
or
G(y) = G(x) + ∇G(x)⊤(y −x) + 1
2(y −x)⊤∇2G(x)(y −x) + O(∥y −x∥3
2).
This theorem indicates that the error in the quadratic approximation is of the order of the
cube of the distance between x and y, making it a more accurate approximation when y is
close to x.

8. Properties of expectation of conditionals. Let x and y be two random variables,
and let h be a function. Show that
(a). Note that E[x | y] is a function of y. However, if x is independent of y, then
E[x | y] = x.
(b). E[c | x] = x, where c is a constant.
(c). Linearity: E[ax1 + bx2 | y] = a · E[x1 | y] + b · E[x2 | y].
(d). Conditional constant: E[h(y)x | y] = h(y)E[x | y], where h(y) is called a condi-
tional constant w.r.t. y.
(e). Monotonicity: if x1 ≤x2, then E[x1 | y] ≤E[x2 | y].
(f). Tower property: E [E[x | y] | h(y)] = E[x | h(y)]; that is, h(y) conveys information
at most as y.

CHAPTER 2. LATENT VARIABLE GENERATIVE MODELS: VAES AND GANS
G
Generative models employ machine learning algorithms to learn a probability
distribution pdata(x) from training data, enabling the synthesis of new, plausible
examples from that distribution. For instance, a generative model trained on
human images learns to generate novel faces by capturing the underlying statistical structure
of the training set. Formally, we can frame such a model as pθ(x), where x denotes a data
vector in the observation space and θ represents the model’s learnable parameters. For
many practical applications, we extend this to conditional generative models of the form
pθ(x | y), where y encodes conditioning variables—such as specifying that a generated face
image should depict a man, a woman, or a child.
Across diverse data modalities, observed data x is often understood as arising from an
associated, unobserved latent variable z, such that the data is generated via p(x | z). A
vivid analogy for this latent variable perspective is Plato’s Allegory of the Cave (Luo, 2022):
prisoners in a cave perceive only two-dimensional shadows cast by unseen three-dimensional
objects, never witnessing the objects themselves. These shadows—their observable reality—
are determined by higher-dimensional abstract entities they cannot directly behold.
Similarly, real-world data can be interpreted as a “projection” of higher-level latent
representations that encode abstract properties (e.g., color, shape, or semantic meaning
in images). Just as cave dwellers infer the existence of hidden objects from shadows, we
aim to approximate these latent representations that generate our observations. A critical
caveat to this analogy, however, is that in generative modeling—unlike Plato’s allegory—we
typically seek lower-dimensional latent representations, as higher-dimensional latents
are impractical to learn without strong priors. This dimensionality reduction acts as a form
of compression, often revealing semantically meaningful structure underlying the observed
data.
With this latent variable framework established, we now turn to the foundational infer-
ence and optimization techniques that underpin modern latent variable generative models.
Directly maximizing the likelihood of observed data pθ(x) is often intractable, as it requires
integrating over the unobserved latent variable z:
pθ(x) =
Z
pθ(x | z) pθ(z) dz.
This integral is typically high-dimensional and cannot be computed in closed form for most
practical models. To address this challenge, we first introduce the evidence lower-bound
(ELBO), a tractable surrogate objective that forms the basis for variational inference (VI),
and we connect it to the expectation-maximization (EM) algorithm—a classic approach for
maximum likelihood estimation with latent variables. Together, these tools provide the
mathematical foundation for learning both the latent distribution pθ(z) and the generative
mapping pθ(x | z), which we will later build upon in other deep generative models.
Building on this latent variable intuition, we next consider autoencoders—a class of
neural networks that learn to compress data into a low-dimensional latent space and then
reconstruct the original input from this compressed representation. While standard au-
toencoders learn a deterministic mapping from data to latents, they lack a probabilistic
interpretation of the latent space, limiting their utility as generative models. To address
this, we introduce the variational autoencoder (VAE), which frames the autoencoder within
the probabilistic latent variable framework we established: it treats the latent space as a
probability distribution p(z), uses variational inference to approximate the intractable pos-


36
2.1. BACKGROUND: ELBO, EM, AND VI
terior p(z | x), and maximizes the ELBO to jointly learn the encoder (inference model) and
decoder (generative model). This marriage of neural networks and probabilistic inference
gives VAEs their powerful generative capabilities, setting the stage for our later discussion
of adversarial generative networks (GANs) as an alternative approach to latent variable
modeling.
As illustrated in Figure 2.1, this core intuition frames generative modeling fundamentally
as a distribution transformation problem: the model learns to map a simple, predefined
latent distribution p(z) (represented by the compact latent space z) to the complex, high-
dimensional data distribution pdata(x) (the manifold of real-world observations, such as
images) via a learned transformation G(z) = x. This transformation aligns samples from
the latent space with the structure of real data, enabling the synthesis of new, plausible
examples that match the target distribution.

2.1. Background: ELBO, EM, and VI
This section presents the foundational principles of variational inference (VI or variational
Bayesian inference), the evidence lower-bound (ELBO), and expectation-maximization
(EM). Together, these concepts establish the mathematical framework underlying mod-
ern latent-variable generative models, including variational autoencoders (VAEs; see Sec-
tion 2.2), and diffusion models (see Chapter 3). Variational inference is an approximate
inference method that seeks to closely match the intractable true posterior distribution
by minimizing a statistical divergence between the learned approximation and the target
posterior.
In VI, this divergence is quantified by the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence,
which measures how much one probability distribution deviates from a reference distribu-
tion. By reformulating Bayesian inference as an optimization problem—namely, minimizing
KL divergence—VI optimizes the parameters of a simple, tractable surrogate distribution,
termed the variational distribution, to best approximate the analytically intractable pos-
terior. This formulation achieves a principled balance between approximation quality and


37
CHAPTER 2. LATENT VARIABLE GENERATIVE MODELS: VAES AND GANS

jection of a cylinder onto two
perpendicular planes (the walls
of a corner), clearly showing
how a single 3D object can ap-
pear as two distinct 2D shapes
when viewed from orthogonal
directions.
computational efficiency, yielding scalable inference solutions for complex probabilistic mod-
els (Jordan et al., 1999; Wainwright et al., 2008).
2.1.1 Motivating Model: Latent Variables and Alternating Methods
Consider a statistical model that jointly generates two random vectors, x ∈X and z ∈Z, in-
stead of one, following a distribution from the parametric family F = {pθ = pθ(·, ·) | θ ∈Θ}.
Formally, (x, z) ∼pθ∗for some true parameter θ∗∈Θ. In practice, however, only re-
alizations of x are observable, where x ∼pdata(x), while the components of z remain
unobserved.
Specifically, although the unknown true parameter θ∗generates N pairs
i.i.d.
pairs (x1, z1), (x2, z2), . . . , (xN, zN), we only have access to the observed dataset
X = {x1, x2, . . . , xN}. The unobserved components z are therefore termed latent vari-
ables or hidden variables. For instance, latent variables may correspond to projections of
a cylinder from two viewpoints, which can be used to reconstruct the original cylinder; see
Figure 2.2 (similar to the “Plato’s Allegory of the Cave”). Other common examples include
the component indicators in Gaussian and Bernoulli mixture models (see Problems 2.8–2.9)
1. Latent variables are integral to model formulation but absent from observational data.
Though unmeasurable, they are critical for explaining the intrinsic structural patterns and
statistical variability of observed data.
Within this framework, the model parameter θ is typically interpreted as a global latent
variable, as it governs the overall data-generating process and influences all observations
and their corresponding latent variables. In contrast, the sample-specific hidden variables
{zn}N
n=1 are referred to as local latent variables (or simply latent variables). Each zn is
uniquely associated with its corresponding observation xn and captures the local variation
specific to that individual data point. This hierarchical relationship between global pa-
rameters, local latent variables, and observed data is illustrated in the graphical model in
Figure 2.3.
Latent variables naturally emerge in a wide range of real-world applications. In cluster-
ing tasks, for example, they correspond to the unknown cluster assignments of individual
data points (Beal, 2003; Jain et al., 2017; Lu, 2021c). In topic modeling, latent variables en-
code the implicit semantic topics underlying document collections, while in image analysis,
they capture high-level visual structures and abstract features that cannot be directly ob-
1. While more complex generative structures are possible, we restrict our attention here to the standard
setting in which each observed data point is associated with its own latent variable.

▶Alternating maximization (AM) algorithm.
To overcome this intractability, a
widely used strategy is the alternating maximization (AM) approach. This iterative method
alternates between two steps: (1) inferring latent variables given the current parameter
estimate, and (2) updating model parameters using the inferred latent variables.
More
concretely, suppose the true parameter θ∗were known. We could then estimate each latent
variable via maximum a posteriori (MAP) assignment:
(AM-Step 1):

latent variables. This alternating procedure yields a practical algorithm for fitting latent
variable models; see Algorithm 1.

or the likelihood itself. These methods yield tractable solutions even in high-dimensional or complex
latent spaces.

▶Limitation and EM algorithm.
Although intuitive, this alternating maximization
framework has significant drawbacks—especially when the latent space Z is large or highly
structured.
At each iteration t, the algorithm performs a “hard assignment,” mapping
each data point xn to a single latent value bz(t)
n
∈Z (we use the superscript (t) to denote
the iteration index). This discards other plausible latent configurations z′ for which the
posterior probability pθ(t)(z′ | xn) may remain substantial, even if lower than the maximum
value pθ(t)(z(t)
n
| xn). As a result, valuable uncertainty information is lost, which can lead
to suboptimal or unstable parameter estimates.
The expectation-maximization (EM) algorithm is designed to address this issue (Baum
et al., 1970; Dempster et al., 1977). Instead of making hard assignments, EM preserves
uncertainty by computing the expected complete-data log-likelihood under the current pos-
terior distribution over latent variables. This “soft assignment” weights all possible latent
values by their posterior probabilities, enabling the algorithm to account for the full range
of plausible interpretations for each observation.
Consequently, EM generally produces
more robust and accurate parameter estimates than basic alternating maximization. To
achieve this, the EM algorithm alternately maximizes the evidence lower-bound (ELBO)
(via coordinate ascent), which—as its name suggests—is a lower bound of the evidence.

2.1.2 ELBO and VFE
As noted earlier, similar to alternating maximization for latent variable models, the EM
algorithm also aims to increase the marginal likelihood over successive iterations. However,
directly maximizing the marginal likelihood (also called model evidence; see Equation (2.1))
is often intractable or computationally prohibitive. To overcome this issue, the EM algo-
rithm introduces a proxy function that acts as a tractable lower bound on the marginal
likelihood. The core idea of EM is to construct such a proxy, referred to as the Q-function,
which lower-bounds the log-marginal likelihood and reformulates the original parameter
estimation problem as a bivariate optimization over both the model parameter θ and an
auxiliary distribution over the latent variables.
To derive the EM algorithm, we assume—without loss of generality—that the latent
variables are supported on a continuous domain.
For a latent variable model, the log-

is known as the evidence lower-bound

hindsight (in the context of constrained EM optimization or variational inference), our goal
is to maximize the ELBO while keeping θ fixed. The first term in the ELBO encourages
the variational distributions qzn(zn) to place high probability on latent configurations that
accurately explain the observed data (the expected energy); while the second term—the
entropy—promotes uncertainty by spreading probability mass over multiple configurations,
thus avoiding overconfident approximations.

▶ELBO as a proxy.
Maximizing the ELBO F(q, θ) with respect to both the model
parameter θ and the approximate posterior distributions qzn(zn) serves two complementary
objectives:
(i) Maximizing the ELBO minimizes the KL divergence between qzn(zn) and pθ(zn | xn).
For fixed θ, the log-likelihood ln pθ(X) is a constant. Maximizing the ELBO F (with
respect to q) is therefore exactly equivalent to minimizing the KL divergence:
max
q
F(q, θ)
⇐⇒
min
q
DKL [qzn(zn) ∥pθ(zn | xn)] .
6. In the deep learning community, the quantity pθ(zn | xn) is commonly referred to as a recognition
network or inference network, which can be parameterized via neural networks (Hinton et al., 1995).
Accordingly, the variational distribution qzn(zn) is optimized to approximate this inference network.


43
CHAPTER 2. LATENT VARIABLE GENERATIVE MODELS: VAES AND GANS
This implies that the approximate posterior q becomes as close as possible to the true
posterior pθ(z | x) or pθ(zn | xn) for each observed data point.
(ii) Maximizing the ELBO tightens the lower bound on the log-likelihood.
Since the
ELBO is a lower bound for ln pθ(X), increasing the ELBO raises this lower bound,
which indirectly promotes an increase in the true log-likelihood.
In practice, we optimize both θ and q in tandem:
For fixed q: Optimize θ to maximize the ELBO, which pushes up the log-likelihood
of the data under the model.
For fixed θ: Optimize q to maximize the ELBO, which is equivalent to minimizing
the KL divergence between q and the true posterior pθ(zn | xn).
When the ELBO is maximized, the KL divergence term becomes zero, and the ELBO equals
the true log-likelihood. At this optimum, the approximate posterior q matches the true
posterior pθ(zn | xn) exactly. See Section 2.1.3 for the unconstrained EM case (in which
case the variational distribution qzn(zn) is equivalent to the true posterior distribution
pθ(zn | xn) at each iteration).
The ELBO decomposition in (2.6b) is also key to extending the unconstrained EM
algorithm to the constrained EM algorithm (in which case the true posterior distribution
pθ(zn | xn) is intractable, and the variational distribution qzn(zn) ≜q(zn | xn, λn) is
restricted to a tractable family of distributions parameterized by λn). We briefly explain
the motivation for maximizing the ELBO in constrained EM algorithms; see Section 2.1.4
for further details. Having introduced latent variables zn for each sample n, our goal is to
infer the underlying latent structure that explains the observed data. In other words, we
aim to optimize the parameters of the variational posterior qzn(zn) ≜q(zn | xn, λn)—where
each data-point-specific variational distribution depends on the variational parameter λn—
to match the true posterior pθ(zn | xn) exactly. This is achieved by minimizing their KL
divergence (ideally to zero). Unfortunately, direct minimization of this KL divergence is
intractable, as the true posterior pθ(zn | xn) is unavailable. However, on the left-hand side
of (2.6b), the data likelihood xn (and thus the evidence term ln pθ(xn)) is constant with
respect to λn. This is because it is computed by marginalizing the latent variables zn from
the joint distribution p(xn, zn) and is entirely independent of λn. Since the ELBO and the
KL divergence sum to a constant, maximizing the ELBO with respect to λn necessarily
minimizes the KL divergence by the same amount. The ELBO can therefore be maximized
as a proxy for learning the true latent posterior: the more we optimize the ELBO, the closer
the approximate posterior becomes to the true posterior. Furthermore, after training, the
ELBO can be used to estimate the likelihood of observed or generated data, as it is trained
to approximate the model evidence ln p(xn).
▶KL divergence behavior.
The direction of the KL divergence strongly influences the
behavior of the variational approximation:
Minimizing DKL[q ∥p] (the reverse or exclusive KL) produces mode-seeking or zero-
forcing behavior: q is forced to zero wherever p is zero, often concentrating its mass
around a single mode of p or one of the modes of p.


44
2.1. BACKGROUND: ELBO, EM, AND VI
In contrast, minimizing DKL[p ∥q] (the forward or inclusive KL) yields mass-covering
or mean-seeking behavior: q must assign non-negligible probability over the entire
support of p, resulting in wider, more inclusive approximations (see Problem 2.3).
2.1.3 EM for Unconstrained Optimization
We now introduce the EM algorithm for latent variable models.
Although its general
formulation is due to Dempster et al. (1977), the core idea had already appeared in earlier
work addressing specific problems (Baum et al., 1970). Similar to alternating maximization,
the EM algorithm iterates between two steps: the E-step, which computes the posterior
distribution of the latent variables given the current parameter estimate, and the M-step,
which updates the model parameters (or global latent variables) by maximizing a surrogate
objective derived from the E-step. Using the ELBO derived in (2.5), and denoting the
estimates qz(z) and θ at iteration t as q(t)
z (z) and θ(t), respectively, the E- and M-step

The EM algorithm can thus be interpreted as follows: the E-step approximates the condi-
tional distributions of the local latent variables {zn}, while the M-step updates the global
parameter θ. These two steps are repeated until the sequence {θ(t)} converges. Under
mild regularity conditions, convergence to a local maximum of the marginal likelihood is
guaranteed (Gupta et al., 2011; Jain et al., 2017). Since the log-likelihood may possess
multiple local maxima, it is common practice to run the EM algorithm multiple times from
different initializations θ(1). The final estimate is then selected as the solution that achieves
the highest likelihood across all runs.

▶Role of ELBO, and Q-function.
The EM algorithm is a fundamental optimization
framework for latent variable models, with the ELBO serving as its core driving mechanism.
Specifically, the E-step tightens the ELBO bound to match the current model likelihood,
while the M-step maximizes the ELBO to update model parameters. This two-stage it-
erative process ensures monotonic improvement and steady convergence toward a local
maximum of the marginal likelihood function. The complete EM procedure is summarized
in Algorithm 2:
1. E-step. Compute the posterior distributions of the latent variables {zn} conditioned
on the observations {xn} and current parameter estimate θ = θ(t). Based on the de-
composition ln pθ(X) = ELBO+KL (see Figure 2.4), since ln pθ(t)(X) is constant with
respect to q, maximizing ELBO is equivalent to minimizing the total KL divergence

data log-likelihood or the Q-function Q(θ | θ(t)).
Because the E-step often admits a closed-form solution (as shown above), the EM algo-
rithm effectively reduces to iteratively constructing and optimizing the Q-function. The
Q-function can be further compactly written as

The Q-function exhibits favorable theoretical properties as a surrogate objective. Crit-
ically, any parameter update that increases Q(θ | θ(t)) is guaranteed to increase the log-
marginal likelihood L (θ). Furthermore, for widely used models including Gaussian mixture
models and mixture regression (see Problems 2.8–2.9), the Q-function can be constructed
and optimized efficiently in closed form (Jain et al., 2017; Lu, 2023).

Rather than

the full posterior distribution pθ(t)(zn | xn) to weight all plausible latent configurations.

In comparison, the alternating maximization algorithm (Algorithm 1) relies on hard
assignment: it selects only the single most probable latent state bzn under the current
posterior for parameter updates. By contrast, the EM algorithm leverages the full posterior
uncertainty, yielding more robust, stable parameter updates—particularly for multimodal
or highly uncertain latent posterior distributions.
▶Stochastic EM algorithm.
In large-scale data scenarios, evaluating the full Q-function
across all N data points in every EM iteration incurs prohibitive computational costs. To
resolve this issue, the stochastic EM algorithm is adopted, which approximates the maxi-
mization (M) step using a single randomly sampled data point or a small mini-batch. At
iteration t, after randomly sampling a data index n, the parameter update proceeds as
follows:
θ(t+1) ←θ(t) + ηt∇Qxn(θ(t) | θ(t)),
(2.14)
where ηt > 0 denotes the step size at iteration t, and ∇Qxn(θ(t) | θ(t)) represents a gradient
ascent direction. On expectation, taking a positive step along this gradient increases the
expected log-likelihood of the model (Lu, 2022a).
▶Maximum a posteriori (MAP EM).
To incorporate prior domain knowledge into
parameter estimation, we impose a prior distribution p(θ) over the model parameter and
maximize the log-posterior objective instead of the standard log-marginal likelihood. The
MAP parameter estimate is defined as:
bθMAP = arg max

We demonstrate the practical implementation of the EM algorithm using the Gaussian
mixture model (GMM), a canonical and widely adopted statistical model. 7
GMMs are
extensively utilized in clustering, density estimation, and topic modeling (where they extract
latent semantic topics from document corpora).

matrices, and non-Gaussian mixture distributions, are explored in Problems 2.8–2.9.

2.1.4 EM for Constrained Optimization
The standard unconstrained EM algorithm yields the exact latent posterior during the
E-step, such that q(t+1)
zn
(zn) = pθ(t)(zn | xn), ∀n (see (2.9)). In practical scenarios, how-
ever, this approach faces critical limitations: real-world data is often governed by multiple
interacting latent variables, rendering the true latent posterior pθ(t)(zn | xn), ∀n computa-
tionally intractable (Williams and Hinton, 1991; Ghahramani and Jordan, 1995; Beal, 2003;
Turner and Sahani, 2011). To resolve this issue, variational Bayesian methods constrain the
posterior to a tractable distribution family, giving rise to the constrained EM algorithm,
also widely known as the variational EM (VEM) algorithm.
Suppose we constrain the approximate posterior to a parametric family Q = {qz(z | λ) =
q(z | λ) | λ ∈Λ}, where λ is called the variational parameter. In practice, the variational
distribution for each latent variable zn is conditioned on its corresponding observation xn.
We thus write q(zn | λn), where λn = {xn, λn}, and only the learnable component λn is
updated during model inference. For simplicity, we denote and optimize over the unified
variational parameter λ ∈Λ throughout our analysis. At iteration t, the E-step becomes
(using Equation (2.6a), which shows that maximizing the ELBO is equivalent to minimizing
the KL divergence when fixing the model parameter θ = θ(t)):
E-Step:
q(t+1)(zn | λn) ←arg max
λ∈Λ
F

n=1 , θ(t)
,

In other words, we seek the variational posterior q(t+1)(· | λn) that is closest—in KL
divergence—to the true (but intractable) posterior pθ(t)(zn | xn).
Since the predefined
variational family Q does not generally include the exact true posterior, the ELBO is no
longer a tight bound. A nonnegative gap persists between the ELBO value and the log-
marginal likelihood of the data.
The M-step retains the standard update rule of unconstrained EM (see (2.11)) but
adopts the optimized variational posterior in place of the intractable exact posterior:

A natural question arises: how can we minimize the KL divergence when the true pos-
terior pθ(t)(zn | xn) is analytically intractable?
The core solution lies in the structural
properties of well-designed variational families. If the variational distribution q(z | λ) sup-
ports tractable moment computation (e.g., Gaussian distributions, fully specified by first-
and second-order moments), the optimization process only requires expectations evaluated
under the true posterior, such as E[zn | xn, θ(t)] and E[znz⊤
n | xn, θ(t)]. We elaborate on
this mechanism in greater detail in the following sections.
