▶Modified generator loss.
Many other modifications to the GAN loss function and
training algorithm have been introduced to stabilize and improve training (Mescheder et al.,
2018). A widely adopted adjustment involves replacing the generator term in the original
loss function
max
Gθ −Eplatent(z)[ln(1 −Dλ(Gθ(zn)))]
(2.95)
with the following alternative formulation:
max
Gθ Eplatent(z)[ln Dλ(Gθ(zn))].
(2.96)
While the first expression minimizes the likelihood that a generated sample is classified
as fake, the second maximizes the probability that it is classified as real.
The distinct
characteristics of these two formulations can be explained using Figure 2.18. When the
generator distribution pG(x) differs substantially from the true data distribution pdata(x),
the value Dλ(Gθ(z)) becomes near zero. In this regime, the original form yields an extremely
small gradient, while the modified form produces a large gradient, resulting in more rapid
learning.
In rare instances, the two loss functions can be combined to maintain a large gradient
at both extremes, as illustrated by the red curve in Figure 2.18:
max
Gθ Eplatent(z)[1
2 ln Dλ(Gθ(zn)) −1
2 ln(1 −Dλ(Gθ(zn)))].
(2.97)
▶Wasserstein GANs.
A more direct approach to encourage the generator distribu-
tion pG(x) to converge toward the data distribution pdata(x) is to adjust the loss function


87
CHAPTER 2. LATENT VARIABLE GENERATIVE MODELS: VAES AND GANS
to directly quantify the distance between these two distributions in the data space. This
separation can be measured using the Wasserstein distance, also referred to as the earth
mover’s distance. Intuitively, we can visualize the distribution pG(x) as a pile of soil that is
gradually relocated in small amounts to form the target distribution pdata(x). The Wasser-
stein metric corresponds to the total mass of soil moved multiplied by the average distance
it is transported. Among all possible ways to reshape the soil pile to match pdata(x), the
Wasserstein distance is defined by the plan that minimizes this average moving distance.
In practice, this distance cannot be computed directly, so it is approximated using
a discriminator network with real-valued outputs. Early approximations constrained the
gradient ∇xDλ(x) of the discriminator with respect to the input x using weight clipping,
leading to the original Wasserstein GAN (Arjovsky et al., 2017). A more robust refinement
introduces an explicit gradient penalty, resulting in the gradient-penalized Wasserstein GAN
(Gulrajani et al., 2017), whose loss function is defined as follows:
JWGAN(λ, θ) = −Epdata(x)




3. Exclusive/inclusive KL. When fitting a parametric distribution qλ to a target
distribution p by minimizing the KL divergence DKL[qλ ∥p] with respect to λ, this is

KL. Show that this leads to mass-covering (or mean-seeking) behavior: qλ must assign

CHAPTER 3. DIFFUSION MODELS
W
We observe that a highly effective strategy for building expressive generative
models is to define a distribution p(z) over a latent variable z, which is then
mapped to the data space x via a deep neural network (see Figure 2.1 for an il-
lustration). This framework allows p(z) to adopt a simple, fixed form—such as the standard
Gaussian distribution N(0, I)—since the transformative flexibility of deep neural networks
enables general-purpose modeling of complex data distributions over x. In Chapter 2, we
covered two prominent instantiations of this modeling framework: variational autoencoders
(VAEs) and generative adversarial networks (GANs), each adopting unique strategies for
neural network design and training.
This chapter elaborates on another class of models within the same general framework:
diffusion models, formally termed denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) (Sohl-
Dickstein et al., 2015; Ho et al., 2020). These models have emerged as the state-of-the-art
approach across numerous generative tasks. Widely deployed in practical systems includ-
ing Stable Diffusion and DALL-E 2 for image generation (Rombach et al., 2022; Ramesh
et al., 2021, 2022) and WaveGrad for speech synthesis (Chen et al., 2020), DDPMs are gen-
erative models that learn to systematically remove noise from data through a controlled,
probabilistic iterative process. DDPM operation consists of two core phases:
Forward diffusion (noising).
Beginning with clean, real-world data (e.g., images,
audio), small increments of Gaussian noise are repeatedly added across numerous
timesteps until the original data signal is completely obscured, leaving only pure ran-
dom Gaussian noise. This forward noising process is fixed, deterministic in procedure,
and fully probabilistic in its output distribution.
Reverse diffusion (denoising). A neural network is trained to predict and subtract
the injected noise step by step. Starting from pure random noise, the model performs
iterative denoising to recover realistic data samples that align with the distribution
of the training dataset.
Unlike VAEs, which generate data via single-step latent decoding, diffusion models
can be interpreted as a hierarchical variant of VAEs.
In this formulation, the encoder
distribution is fixed by the predefined forward noising process, while only the generative
reverse distribution is learned end-to-end. Diffusion models therefore synthesize new data
samples through continuous, multi-step stochastic evolution.
Diffusion models offer distinct practical advantages: they feature simplified training
pipelines, scale efficiently on parallel computing hardware, and eliminate the training in-
stabilities and optimization challenges inherent to adversarial training. Empirically, they
produce generative outputs matching or exceeding the quality of GAN-generated samples.
Their primary limitation lies in computational efficiency: the multi-step iterative denois-
ing process requires numerous forward passes through the neural network, increasing the
computational cost of sample generation.
3.1. Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DPMs)
The release of OpenAI’s DALL-E series (Ramesh et al., 2021; Nichol et al., 2021; Ramesh
et al., 2022) has greatly popularized high-fidelity image generation, bringing diffusion model
algorithms to widespread attention among researchers and industry practitioners. Originally
proposed in 2015, diffusion models draw core inspiration from non-equilibrium thermody-


94
3.1.
DIFFUSION PROBABILISTIC MODELS (DPMS)
namics (Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015). This chapter systematically introduces the fundamen-
tal principles of diffusion models.
3.1.1 Diffusion Probabilistic Model
Diffusion probabilistic models (DPMs), also simply referred to as diffusion models (DMs),
were first proposed by Sohl-Dickstein et al. (2015) in 2015. The original work motivates
the model design through non-equilibrium statistical physics. In this chapter, we introduce
and analyze DPMs from a probabilistic perspective.
VAE and Hierarchical VAE
We previously compare standard VAEs and hierarchical VAEs (HVAEs) in Section 2.2.9
and illustrate their structural differences in Figure 2.13.
For consistent and simplified
notation throughout this chapter, we adopt subscripts instead of superscripts to index
sequence dimensions. To clarify the notational convention: prior chapters use subscripts
to index individual data samples within a dataset (i.e., xn ∈X = {x1, x2, . . . , xN}) and
superscripts to denote sequential indices of latent or data variables. The key architectural
distinctions between standard VAEs and HVAEs relevant to DDPM modeling are visualized
in Figure 3.1.

Figure 3.1: Comparison of standard VAE and hierarchical VAE, depicting the hierarchical
VAE framework with T hierarchical latent variables. The generative process is modeled as
a Markov chain, where each latent variable zt is generated only from the preceding latent
variable zt+1. Shaded circles represent observed variables, while unshaded circles correspond
to unobserved latent variables.
From a theoretical perspective, diffusion models can be regarded as an extension of
VAEs. Accordingly, we initiate our analysis from the fundamental VAE framework. We
first review the basic VAE model (see Figure 3.1(a)), which involves two core variables: the
observed variable x and the latent hidden variable z. For a training data sample (e.g., an
input image) denoted as x = x0, we treat the sample as a D-dimensional vector lying in the
space RD. The autoencoder pipeline first encodes the input x0 into a latent representation
z1, and then reconstructs the original input x0 by decoding from z1. Specifically, x0 serves
as the input of the encoder and the output of the decoder, whereas z1 acts as the encoder
output and the decoder input.
As visualized in the figure, the conditional distribution
pθ(x0 | z1) characterizes the decoding process, and qλ(z1 | x0) corresponds to the encoding
process.
A standard VAE performs only a single encoding step. This naturally raises a question:
can the encoding process be extended to multiple iterative steps? As illustrated in Fig-
ure 3.1(b), extending the single-step VAE encoding to multi-step iterations yields a Markov


95
CHAPTER 3. DIFFUSION MODELS
chain-like chained structure with bidirectional propagation. The left-to-right propagation
corresponds to a progressive encoding process, equivalent to iteratively executing the VAE
encoder T times. Conversely, the right-to-left propagation represents a progressive decod-
ing process, which is equivalent to T iterative executions of the VAE decoder. We define
the multi-step encoding procedure as the forward process (or forward trajectory) and the
multi-step decoding procedure as the reverse process (or reverse trajectory).
Both the forward and reverse processes satisfy the Markov property, where the state at
each timestep t depends solely on the state at the previous timestep. In this framework,
q(zt | zt−1) denotes a single-step encoding operation, and p(zt−1 | zt) denotes a single-
step decoding operation.
All latent variables across timesteps z1:T (where z1:T denotes
z1, z2, . . . , zT ) collectively form the complete latent variable set of the hierarchical model.
The joint probability distribution of the entire hierarchical VAE model is formulated as:
pθ(x0, z1:T ) = p(zT )

noise corruption introduced during the multi-step encoding process (detailed in subsequent
discussions).
The posterior distribution of the latent variables qλ(z1:T | x0) conditioned on the ob-
served input x0 can be decomposed into a product of conditional transition distributions:

Diffusion Model
Extending the standard VAE to a Markovian chain structure yields the Markovian hierar-
chical VAE (MHVAE), which serves as the fundamental prototype of diffusion models. In
fact, diffusion models can be obtained by introducing minor modifications to the MHVAE
framework. The core differences between diffusion models and MHVAE lie in the following
three aspects:


96
3.1.
DIFFUSION PROBABILISTIC MODELS (DPMS)

x T
Forward Process

x T
Figure 3.2: Conceptual illustration of the comparison between VAE and diffusion models.
(A1) Dimension consistency.
Each step of the forward and backward processes adopts
identical input and output dimensions, such that all latent variables zt share the
same dimensionality as the original input x0. 1 This setup differs from conventional
VAEs, where the latent dimension of z is typically smaller than the dimension of the
observed input x.
(A2) Fixed forward transformation. In the multi-step forward encoding process, the encoder
distribution q(zt | zt−1) = qλ(zt | zt−1) is no longer a learnable neural network
parameterized by λ. Instead, it is fixed as a predefined linear Gaussian transformation,
whose explicit formulation is presented in the subsequent content.
(A3) Standard normal prior asymptotics.
Benefiting from the linear Gaussian encoder
constraint and the inherent Markov chain properties, the latent variable zT asymp-
totically obeys a Gaussian distribution as the total timestep T →∞. In other words,
zT converges to a Gaussian distribution with increasing T.
By imposing a decay
factor smaller than 1 on the linear Gaussian transformation, zT can be guaranteed
to converge to the standard normal distribution, namely p(zT ) ∼N(0, ID) in (3.1),
where 0 denotes the zero mean and ID denotes the isotropic unit covariance matrix.
An intuitive conceptual comparison between standard VAEs and diffusion models is pro-
vided in Figure 3.2.
3.1.2 Forward and Reverse Processes
The process of a diffusion model is illustrated in Figure 3.3, which corresponds to a Bayesian
network (directed graphical model) with a Markov chain structure. The variable x = x0
serves as the model’s starting point and represents the distribution of real-world data (e.g.,
real images). Starting from x0, the data distribution gradually evolves into a standard
Gaussian distribution zT following this Markov chain structure. The joint probability dis-
tribution of the entire graphical network is formulated as pθ(x0, z1:T ). Given the observed
data vector x = x0 where x ∼pdata(x), the conditional joint distribution of the latent
variables z1:T is defined as:
q(z1, . . . , zt | x0) = q(z1 | x0)

Figure 3.3: Graphical representation of the diffusion model. The original data x = x0 is
shown by the shaded node, since it is an observed variable, whereas the noise-corrupted data
z1, . . . , zT are considered to be latent variables. The noise process is defined by the forward
distribution q(zt | zt−1) and can be viewed as an encoder. Our goal is to learn a model
pθ(zt−1 | zt) that tries to reverse this noise process and which can be viewed as a decoder.
As we will see later, the conditional distribution q(zt−1 | zt, x0) plays an important role in
defining the training procedure.
This left-to-right Markovian evolution is termed the forward process (also referred to as the
diffusion process, forward diffusion, or forward noising). 2 The forward process iteratively
injects noise into the real data sample x0, ultimately converting it into a pure Gaussian
random variable zT : zT ∼N(0, ID), 3 where T denotes the total number of diffusion
steps, which corresponds to the number of noise injection operations in the forward process
and matches the number of denoising steps required for the subsequent reverse process, as
elaborated below and visualized in Figure 3.3.
In contrast, the right-to-left evolution in Figure 3.3 constitutes the inverse of the forward
diffusion process and is thus defined as the reverse process (or denoising process, reverse
diffusion). This process progressively transforms the standard Gaussian noise variable zT
into a realistic data sample x0. In generative AI, this transformation pipeline corresponds
to novel image generation, hence it is also known as the image generation process. Sta-
tistically, this procedure essentially implements sampling from the target joint probability
distribution, and is therefore additionally referred to as the sampling process. Consistent
with this reverse evolution order, the joint distribution pθ(x0, z1:T ) can be factorized as
follows (same as the HVAE formulation (3.1)):
pθ(x0, z1:T ) = p(zT )

observation as x = x0. The true probability density function of the real data distribution
pdata(x0) remains unknown. However, we can access a collection of real data samples, which

Following Assumption (A2), each latent variable zt follows a Gaussian distribution,
and the forward encoder distribution q(zt | zt−1) corresponds to a fixed linear Gaussian
transformation. Specifically, a linear Gaussian transformation implies that the mean of zt
is a linear function of the preceding latent variable zt−1. Given a training data sample
x = x0, we corrupt the data by injecting independent Gaussian noise into each dimension
of the vector. Treating the input data as a D-dimensional vector x0 ∈RD, the resulting
noisy variable z1 ∈RD is defined as:
z1 =

where β1 < 1 controls the noise variance at the first diffusion step. The weighted coefficients
√1 −β1 and √β1 in (3.7a) or (3.8a) are carefully designed. They ensure that the mean of
zt gradually approaches zero and its variance gradually approaches the identity matrix as
the diffusion step progresses.
This noise injection procedure is repeated iteratively with independent Gaussian noise
at each step, generating a sequence of progressively noisier variables z2, . . . , zT . Given a
predefined noise scheduler (or variance scheduler) β1, β2, . . . , βT , the update rule for each
subsequent noisy latent variable is formulated as:
zt =

where the mean µzt of the conditional distribution q(zt | zt−1) is strictly a linear function of
zt−1. Equation (3.8b) indicates that each forward step essentially adds zero-mean Gaussian
noise with covariance βtID to the scaled version of the previous latent variable √1 −βtzt−1.
The sequence of conditional distributions defined in (3.8b) forms a well-defined Markov
chain, which can be represented as the probabilistic graphical model illustrated in Fig-
ure 3.3. As the step index t increases, the latent variable zt gradually degrades into pure
Gaussian noise. As visualized in Figure 3.3 and Figure 3.4, clean input images are pro-
gressively corrupted until they become indistinguishable from random Gaussian noise. This
degradation process can be intuitively analogized to ink diffusing in water. Initially con-
centrated ink gradually spreads uniformly throughout the liquid over time, rendering the
original ink droplet unrecognizable and the entire solution uniformly turbid. This diffusion-
inspired mechanism gives the model its name: the diffusion models or diffusion probabilistic
models (DPMs).
4. Note again that, consistent with standard diffusion model literature, latent variables are occasionally
denoted as x1, . . . , xT in other works, with x0 reserved for the observed data. This book adopts z for
latent variables and x for observed variables to maintain notational consistency throughout the text.

Figure 3.4: Diffusion step for a set of images with t = 0, 10, 30, 50, 500, 999 steps. In
the process, we adopt a linear variance schedule for the forward diffusion process (i.e.,
βt = β1+ t−1
T−1(βT −β1), for t = 1, 2, . . . , T and T = 1000), following the protocol established
by Ho et al. (2020). This schedule ensures that the signal is gradually obscured by Gaussian
noise, transitioning from nearly identity transformations at early steps to near-pure noise
at the final step T.
To guarantee that the latent mean converges to zero (µzt →0), all variance parameters
are constrained to βt ∈(0, 1). These hyperparameters can be manually predefined and
follow a monotonic increasing schedule: β1 < β2 < . . . < βT < 1.
5
Intuitively, small
noise variance is applied at early diffusion steps, resulting in mild data corruption and
slow diffusion. As the step proceeds, increasing noise variance amplifies corruption and
accelerates the diffusion process.
The monotonic increasing design of the {βt} schedule
offers two core advantages:
Large noise magnitudes in early steps would cause drastic, irreversible distortion to
the original data distribution, making it infeasible for the reverse denoising process to
recover authentic signals.
Insufficient noise injection in late-stage steps (when data is already nearly randomized)
leads to negligible distribution changes, slowing convergence and requiring excessive
steps to achieve full Gaussianization.
The increasing variance schedule enables a slow-start and accelerating diffusion trajectory,
which is optimal for stable and complete forward corruption. In summary, the forward
diffusion process iteratively superimposes calibrated Gaussian noise on the original data
until the structured data distribution evolves into pure standard Gaussian noise. Critically,
the forward encoder distribution q(zt | zt−1) is non-trainable; it is fixed as a predefined
linear Gaussian transformation throughout training and inference.
Forward Process: Diffusion Kernel
To compute zt at each timestep of the forward diffusion process, the most straightforward
approach is sequential iteration from x0 to zT using the transition formula in (3.8b). At each
iteration, the noise term ϵt is sampled from a standard normal distribution, following the
noise sampling strategy used in standard VAEs (see Algorithm 3). While this iterative up-
date scheme is mathematically valid, it suffers from low computational efficiency, especially
when the total number of diffusion steps T is large. By exploiting the linear Gaussian prop-
erty of the transition distribution q(zt | zt−1), we derive a closed-form solution that directly
5. The βt coefficients can be either manually fixed as static hyperparameters (Ho et al., 2020) or optimized
as learnable model parameters (Kingma et al., 2021).

computes zt from the original input data x0 for any arbitrary timestep t. This closed-form
expression supports parallel computation of all intermediate latent states {zt} and sig-
nificantly accelerates the forward diffusion process. Specifically, the joint distribution of the
latent variables, conditioned on the observed data vector x = x0 (x ∼pdata(x)), is given
by

After sufficiently many steps, the raw data becomes indistinguishable from Gaussian
noise. In the limit T →∞, we have
q(zT | x0) = N(zT | 0, ID)
=⇒
q(zT ) = N(zT | 0, ID).
(3.13)
at which point all information about the original data is lost.
The resulting marginal
distribution for zT follows directly from the fact that the right-hand side expression is
independent of x0.
The choice of coefficients √1 −βt and √βt introduced earlier (e.g.,
in (3.8a)) guarantees that once the Markov chain converges to a zero-mean, unit-covariance
distribution, further updates maintain this stationary distribution.
Reverse Process: Data-Conditioned Distribution
Our objective is to learn to reverse the noise-adding process, so it is natural to examine
the inverse of the conditional distribution q(zt | zt−1), which can be derived via Bayes’
theorem (1.10) as
q(zt−1 | zt) = q(zt | zt−1)q(zt−1)
q(zt)
.
(3.14)
The marginal distribution q(zt−1) can be expressed as
q(zt−1) =
Z
q(zt−1 | x0)pdata(x0) dx0,
(3.15)
where q(zt−1 | x0) corresponds to the conditional Gaussian defined in (3.10a). However,
this distribution is intractable, as it requires integration over the unknown data density
pdata(x0). Approximating this integral using training dataset samples results in a complex
distribution represented as a Gaussian mixture.
Instead, we focus on the data-conditioned reverse distribution q(zt−1 | zt, x0) (see
also Figure 3.3), which we will show shortly follows a simple Gaussian form. Intuitively, this
makes sense: while it is challenging to infer a less-noisy latent state from a noisy observation
alone, the problem becomes far more manageable when the original clean data x0 is also
known. This form will turn out to be useful when we rewrite the ELBO in Section 3.1.3.
This conditional distribution can be derived using Bayes’ theorem (1.10):
q(zt−1 | zt, x0) = q(zt | zt−1, x0)q(zt−1 | x0)
q(zt | x0)
= q(zt | zt−1)q(zt−1 | x0)
q(zt | x0)
,
(3.16)
where the second equality follows from the Markov property of the forward diffusion process.
The quantity q(zt | zt−1) is given by (3.8b); as a function of zt−1, this takes the form of
an exponential of a quadratic form. Similarly, the quantity q(zt−1 | x0) in the numerator
of (3.16) is the diffusion kernel from (3.10a), which also involves an exponential quadratic
in zt−1. The denominator of (3.16) can be disregarded since it is constant with respect
to zt−1.
Consequently, the right-hand side of (3.16) is a Gaussian distribution, whose
mean and covariance can be identified through the method of matching Gaussian sufficient
statistics, yielding
q(zt−1 | zt, x0) = N (zt−1 | µq

Reverse Process: Neural Network Approximation
We have established that the forward encoder is defined by a sequence of Gaussian condi-
tional distributions q(zt | zt−1). As illustrated in Figure 3.3, the reverse process represents
a right-to-left decoding operation. Starting from zT (which corresponds to pure random
Gaussian noise), the model progressively reconstructs meaningful structured data such as
images by iteratively denoising the latent variables. At each timestep t, he objective is to
estimate zt−1 from the noisy latent state zt. The conditional distribution governing this
backward transition is denoted q(zt−1 | zt). Under this reverse process, we factorize the
joint distribution p(x0, z1:T ) as follows:
p(x0, z1:T ) = p(zT )
( 2
Y
t=T
q(zt−1 | zt)
)
q(x0 | z1).
(3.20)
The density of p(zT ) is known analytically and follows a standard multivariate Gaussian
distribution, i.e., p(zT ) ∼N(0, ID). In contrast, the reverse conditional q(zt−1 | zt) is
intractable to compute directly. Even when derived via Bayes’ theorem, its normalization
constant involves integrals without closed-form expressions, making the exact distribution
computationally infeasible to evaluate. Furthermore, it would require integration over all
possible values of the initial vector x0, whose distribution corresponds to the unknown data
distribution pdata(x0) that we aim to model.
▶Neural network approximation.
Instead, we learn an approximation to the reverse
distribution using a parameterized distribution pθ(zt−1 | zt) implemented by a deep neural
network, where θ denotes the network’s weights and biases:
q(zt−1 | zt) ≈pθ(zt−1 | zt).
(3.21)
This reverse step acts analogously to the decoder in a variational autoencoder and is visu-
alized in Figure 3.3.
▶Form of the approximation.
Intuitively, setting small step variances in the forward
process (3.8) such that βt ≪1 , ensures that changes in the latent vector between con-
secutive steps remain modest, which in turn simplifies learning the inverse transformation.
More precisely, if βt ≪1, then the true reverse distribution q(zt−1 | zt) becomes
approximately Gaussian in zt−1. This behavior follows from (3.14), since the right-hand
side depends on zt−1 through q(zt | zt−1) and q(zt−1). If the forward transition distribution
q(zt | zt−1) is a sufficiently narrow Gaussian (i.e., βt ≪1), then the marginal distribution


103
CHAPTER 3.
DIFFUSION MODELS
q(zt−1) carries substantial probability mass only over regions where q(zt | zt−1) is also con-
centrated, implying that q(zt−1 | zt) is likewise approximately Gaussian. This heuristic is
supported by a simple illustrative example shown in Figures 3.6.
However, because the per-step variances are small, a large number of steps is required
to ensure that the latent distribution zT produced by the forward noising process remains
close to a standard Gaussian.
This increases the computational cost of generating new
samples; in practice, T often ranges in the thousands.
q(zt | zt−1)
q(zt−1)

Figure 3.6: Illustration of the reverse distribution q(zt−1 | zt) derived via Bayes’ theo-
rem (3.16) for scalar random variables. Upper. The blue curve on the right shows the
marginal distribution q(zt−1) modeled as a mixture of three Gaussians, while the left panel
displays the forward Gaussian noise process q(zt | zt−1) as a distribution over zt centered
at zt−1. Their product, after normalization, yields the distribution q(zt−1 | zt), plotted in
green for a fixed value of zt. Since the left-hand distribution is relatively broad, correspond-
ing to a large variance βt, the resulting reverse distribution exhibits a complex multimodal
shape. Bottom. The forward distribution q(zt | zt−1) in the left panel has a much smaller
variance βt. The corresponding reverse distribution q(zt−1 | zt), shown in green on the
right, is nearly Gaussian with a variance similar to that of q(zt | zt−1). Adapted from
Bishop and Bishop (2023).

The above discussion motivates approximating the true reverse process q(zt−1 | zt) by
a Gaussian distribution. We can formally verify that the reverse distribution q(zt−1 | zt) is
approximately Gaussian by performing a Taylor series expansion of ln q(zt−1 | zt) around
zt with respect to zt−1. This expansion also demonstrates that for small step variances,
the covariance of the reverse distribution q(zt−1 | zt) is close to the covariance βtID of the
forward noise process q(zt | zt−1).
▶The final Gaussian approximation.
Accordingly, we model the reverse process (or
reverse diffusion) using a Gaussian distribution of the form
pθ(zt−1 | zt) = N

and Dhariwal, 2021). The network explicitly takes the timestep t as input to adapt to the
varying values of βt across different stages of the Markov chain. Similar to the amortization
inference for VAEs (see Figure 2.12), this design allows a single network to invert all steps
of the chain, rather than requiring a separate model for each step.
Considerable flexibility exists in choosing the neural network architecture for µθ
t (zt),
as long as its output matches the input dimensionality. Under this constraint, U-Net or a
diffusion transformer (DiT) architectures are standard choices for image generation tasks
(Ronneberger et al., 2015; Peebles and Xie, 2023). See Chapter 7 for more details.
The complete reverse process approximating (3.20) then forms a Markov chain expressed
as
p(x0, z1:T ) ≈pθ(x0, z1:T ) = p(zT )

(3.23)
where p(zT ) is given by N(zT | 0, ID).
After training, we can sample from the sim-
ple Gaussian prior over zT and map it to a sample from the data distribution pdata(x0)
(approximated as the marginal distribution pθ(x0)) through a sequence of reverse steps ob-
tained by repeatedly applying the trained network. This again reflects the core paradigm of
this book: transforming samples from an initial simple latent distribution (e.g., a Gaussian)
into samples from the target data distribution; see Figure 1.2 for an illustration.
3.1.3 The Objective Function (ELBO)
We now turn to defining an objective function for training the neural network. A standard
approach for learning the unknown parameters of a probabilistic model is maximum like-
lihood estimation (see, for example, (2.1)), which involves maximizing the log-probability
of the observed data point x0. In our case, the full joint distribution of the model is given
pθ(x0, z1:T ). However, we only observe realizations of x0, while the latent variables z1:T
are unobserved. We therefore maximize the marginal distribution pθ(x0) rather than the
joint distribution pθ(x0, z1:T ). The marginal likelihood can be obtained by integrating out
the latent variables:
pθ(x0) =
Z
pθ(x0, z1:T ) dz1:T .
(3.24)


105
CHAPTER 3.
DIFFUSION MODELS
where pθ(x0, z1:T ) is defined in (3.23). This formulation corresponds to the general latent
variable model (LVM) (2.1), where the latent variables are given by Z = {z1, z2, . . . , zT } and
the observed variable is x0. Notably, all latent variables share the same dimensionality as
the data space, a property that does not hold for VAEs or GANs, as discussed in Chapter 2.
Deriving the ELBO for DPMs
From (3.24), we observe that the likelihood requires integration over all possible trajec-
tories that could map Gaussian noise to the observed data point.
These integrals are
intractable, as they involve integration across the highly nonlinear and complex mappings
induced by the neural network. We therefore use an approach analogous to that employed
for VAEs (and hierarchical VAEs), and instead maximize a lower bound on the log-likelihood
known as the evidence lower-bound (ELBO), introduced in Section 2.1.2. We rederive this
bound specifically for diffusion models below. For any valid distribution q(Z), the follow-
ing identity—referred to as the ELBO decomposition—always holds (see equations (2.6b),
(2.53), or Figure 2.4):
ln pθ(x0)

}

KL divergence
,
(3.25)
where F denotes the ELBO, and DKL[P ∥Q] denotes the KL divergence between distribu-
tions P and Q; see (2.3). Since the KL divergence is nonnegative, we have
ln pθ(x0) ≥F(θ).
(3.26)
Given that the exact log-likelihood is intractable, we train the neural network by maximizing
this lower bound F(θ).
To proceed, we first derive an explicit expression for the diffusion model ELBO. Note that
the inequality (3.26) holds for any valid distribution q(Z). In many ELBO applications,
such as the VAE, q(Z) is equipped with learnable parameters λ (q(Z) = qλ(Z | x0),
the encoder of VAEs), typically implemented via a deep neural network, and the ELBO
is maximized with respect to both these parameters and those of the joint distribution
pθ(x0, Z). Optimizing q(Z) tightens the bound, bringing the parameter optimization for
pθ(x0, Z) closer to exact maximum likelihood estimation; see Section 2.2.6.
However, for diffusion models, we set q(Z) to the fixed distribution q(Z) = q(z1, . . . , zT |
x0) given by the forward Markov chain in (3.9). Consequently, the only learnable parame-
ters are those defining the reverse Markov chain model pθ(x0, z1:T ).
We now substitute the forward diffusion process q(z1, . . . , zT | x0) from (3.9) and the
reverse process pθ(x0, z1:T ) from (3.23) into (3.25). This allows us to expand the ELBO as

The first term ln p(zT ) on the right-hand side of (3.27) corresponds to the fixed stan-

as a constant offset and can be dropped from the ELBO without affecting optimization.
Similarly, the fourth term −ln q(z1 | x0) is independent of θ and may also be omitted. The
second term in (3.27) plays the same role as the reconstruction loss in VAEs. Its expec-

by (3.10a), yielding

∼N(z1 | √1 −β1x0, β1ID). In contrast to VAEs, we do not need to backpropa-

the reparameterization trick (see Section 2.2.8) is therefore unnecessary.
The third term on the right-hand side of (3.27) consists of a sum over terms involving
consecutive latent variables zt−1 and zt. Recall from our earlier derivation of the diffusion
kernel (3.10a) that we can directly sample q(zt−1 | x0) as a Gaussian, and then obtain a
corresponding sample of zt via (3.8a) or (3.10b), which is also Gaussian. While this ap-
proach is asymptotically correct, sampling pairs of latent variables produces high-variance
Monte Carlo estimates, requiring an impractically large number of samples to reduce noise.
Equivalently, this third term can be expressed as an expectation of a KL divergence over
two successive random variables:
Eq(zt+1|zt)q(zt−1|zt−2) [DKL (q(zt | zt−1) ∥pθ(zt | zt+1))] .
Sampling both variables simultaneously again introduces high variance, destabilizing opti-
mization and slowing convergence. For these reasons, directly optimizing (3.27) is not ideal.
Instead, we reformulate the ELBO into a form that can be reliably estimated by sampling
only one variable per term.

the ELBO using KL divergences, which will subsequently allow us to express it in closed
form.

q(zt | zt−1), yielding

▶Prior matching term.
The second term is the prior matching term, which takes the
form of a KL divergence and corresponds to the regularization term in the VAE objective
(2.59d). The key distinction is that q(zT | x0) contains no learnable parameters: it repre-
sents the forward process, which we assume to be a known linear Gaussian transformation
and thus requires no model training. When T is sufficiently large, this term approaches zero.
Moreover, as mentioned previously, since it is independent of θ, it can be safely omitted
from the optimization objective.
▶Reconstruction term.
The term Eq(z1|x0) [ln pθ(x0 | z1)] serves as the reconstruction
term, analogous to its counterpart in the VAE objective (2.59d). It reconstructs the orig-
inal data x0 from the first-step latent variable z1. This reconstruction term assigns high
probability to the observed data sample and can be trained using the same Monte Carlo
strategy as the corresponding term in the VAE, via the sampling approximation in (3.29).

kernel (3.10a). Compared to the original ELBO error in (3.27), we now only sample one
variable per term, leading to Monte Carlo estimates with significantly lower variance.
When optimizing the model via stochastic gradient descent (SGD), we compute the
gradient of the loss function with respect to the network parameters for a randomly sampled
training data point x0. Instead of evaluating the loss for every timestep t in the summation
from (3.40), we randomly sample a single timestep along the Markov chain for each training
sample. Gradients are accumulated across mini-batches and subsequently applied to update
the network weights.
Notably, this loss formulation inherently induces implicit data augmentation. For each
training sample x0, a unique noise realization ϵt is sampled in every training iteration,
generating a distinct noisy latent state zt. The above optimization pipeline applies to a
single training sample x0, and the full gradient computation workflow is summarized in
Algorithm 4.

DIFFUSION MODELS
At each timestep t, the model directly predicts the original data x0. Intuitively, this
design appears somewhat unnatural: when t is large, zt is heavily corrupted and far from
x0. Even though t is provided as an input to the model, it remains challenging for a single
parameterization to robustly handle the full range of timesteps. Empirical results confirm
this limitation: early diffusion probabilistic models exhibited relatively weak performance
and low-quality image generation, as documented in comparative experiments (Ho et al.,
2020). Due to these drawbacks, the original DPM formulation proposed in 2015 gained little
mainstream attention. This changed in 2020, when Ho et al. (2020) introduced denoising
diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) as a significantly improved variant. Following this
work, OpenAI and Stability AI released models such as DALL-E 2 and Stable Diffusion built
upon DDPMs (Ramesh et al., 2022; Rombach et al., 2022), enabling breakthrough perfor-
mance and bringing diffusion models widespread recognition. In Section 3.2, we examine
the key innovations introduced by DDPMs.
3.1.5 Extension to Binary Data
Given binary input data x0 ∈{0, 1}D he Gaussian-kernel-based DPM can be readily adapted
to binary data via the binomial kernel, a formulation known as binomial diffusion (Sohl-
Dickstein et al., 2015). In this framework, the forward diffusion process gradually corrupts
the original data to converge to a stationary Bernoulli(1/2) distribution.
▶Forward kernel.
At each diffusion step, every bit is stochastically flipped toward a
uniform binary distribution governed by the noise coefficient βt, following the transition
kernel:
q(zt | zt−1) = Bernoulli
 zt | (1 −βt)zt−1 + 1
2βt

(3.44)
This definition implies that the Bernoulli sampling probability at timestep t is a convex
combination of the previous latent state and the uniform probability 1/2. Note that for a
scalar x ∈{0, 1} and probability parameter p ∈[0, 1], the Bernoulli distribution is formally
defined as:
Bernoulli(x | p) = px(1 −p)1−x =

▶Marginal kernel.
By composing the sequential forward diffusion steps, we define the
cumulative product parameter αt ≜Qt

q(zt | x0) = Bernoulli
 zt | αtx0 + 1

A hint of the derivation is provided in Problem 3.11. As the diffusion step t approaches
the total step T, αt →0, such that the marginal distribution converges to q(zT ) →
Bernoulli(1/2), regardless of the original input x0.
▶Posterior (reverse-time conditional).
Using Bayes’ theorem 1.10 for binary state
spaces, the true data-dependent reverse conditional distribution q(zt−1 | zt, x0) follows a
Bernoulli distribution with the success probability formulated as:
q(zt−1 = 1 | zt, x0) =

distribution serves as the optimization target for the learned reverse diffusion model.

The terminal term JT ≈0 in practice, since the terminal forward distribution q(zT |
x0) asymptotically matches the uniform prior p(zT ) = Bernoulli(1/2).
Each interme-
diate loss term Jt−1 computes the KL divergence between two Bernoulli distributions
DKL[Bernoulli(a) ∥Bernoulli(b)] (see Problem 3.10), with
a = q(zt−1 = 1 | zt, x0): the success probability of the true posterior, computed using
the ground-truth original data x0;
b = q(zt−1 = 1 | zt, bx0): the success probability of the approximate posterior, com-
puted using the model-predicted data bx0.
This framework can be seamlessly extended to high-dimensional binary datasets x0 ∈RD.
A visual illustration of the full pipeline is presented in Figure 3.7.
The final training
objective is obtained by summing or averaging the ELBO loss terms across all diffusion
steps t and all D binary dimensions. For sampling, we first sample the terminal latent
variable zT ∼Bernoulli(1/2), then perform ancestral sampling over the learned reverse
kernel pθ(zt−1 | zt) to recover the original binary data x0.
3.2. Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs)
As mentioned previously, DDPMs introduced key improvements and optimizations to DPMs,
addressing some of their shortcomings and significantly enhancing the quality of data gen-
erated by diffusion models. This is what enables diffusion models to achieve outstanding
performance in the field of image or video generation. The central improvement introduced
by DDPMs is a revision to what the parameterized model predicts:
(A4) Instead of predicting the original x0, the model now predicts the noise added at each
timestep, reducing the difficulty of model learning (Ho et al., 2020).
Why predict noise rather than a less noisy data or the original clean data? The core
motivation stems from the relative learning difficulty of the two prediction tasks. At in-
termediate diffusion steps, the latent variable zt is dominated by noise, especially as t
approaches T. Directly predicting the full clean data x0 or a fully denoised latent state
zt−1 from such a heavily corrupted signal requires the model to recover fine-grained, high-
frequency structural details from extremely noisy inputs—a highly nonlinear and challenging
regression problem. In contrast, the noise ϵt that defines the diffusion kernel is zero-mean,
independent and identically distributed Gaussian noise across all spatial dimensions and
timesteps. Its statistical structure remains simple and consistent throughout the forward
process, making the prediction target considerably more predictable and stable.
Empirically, predicting noise also yields lower-variance gradients during training and
improved convergence behavior. This is because the model learns a signal with a station-
ary, standardized distribution rather than one that varies drastically across timesteps and
spatial regions (e.g., different image regions). Furthermore, formulating the learning objec-
tive around noise prediction simplifies the loss landscape, mitigates strong covariate shift
between early and late diffusion steps, and allows the network to focus on estimating a
well-behaved signal rather than hallucinating entire data structures from heavily obscured
inputs. This reduction in learning difficulty directly translates to higher-quality generated
samples and more stable training dynamics.

3.2. DENOISING DIFFUSION PROBABILISTIC MODELS (DDPMS)
3.2.2 Generating New Samples
Once the network has been trained, we can generate new samples in the data space by first
sampling from the Gaussian distribution p(zT ) ∼N(0, ID) and then performing successive
denoising through each step of the Markov chain. Given a denoised latent sample zt at
step t, we generate a sample zt−1 in three steps. First we evaluate the output of the neural
network, denoted ϵθ
t (zt). From this predicted noise, we evaluate µθ
t (zt) using (3.50). Finally
we generate a sample zt−1 from pθ(zt−1 | zt) = N(zt−1 | µθ
t (zt), (σθ
t )2ID) by adding noise
scaled by the variance so that
zt−1 = µθ
t (zt) + σθ
t ϵ,
where ϵ ∼N(ϵ | 0, ID),
(3.54)
where setting either (σθ
t )2 = βt or (σθ
t )2 = (σq
t )2 yields comparable performance (Ho et al.,
2020). The first choice is optimal when x0 ∼N(0, ID), while the second is optimal when
x0 is deterministically fixed to a single point. These represent two extreme settings corre-
sponding to upper and lower bounds on the entropy of the reverse process for data with
coordinate-wise unit variance (Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015). Note that the network ϵθ
t (·)
predicts the total noise added to the original data vector x0 to produce zt. However, in
the sampling step, we only subtract a fraction βt/√1 −αt of this noise from zt (see (3.50)),
then inject additional noise with variance σθ
t to generate zt−1 (see (3.22)). At the final step,
when computing the synthetic data sample ex0, we do not add extra noise, as our goal is
to produce a noise-free output. The full sampling procedure is summarized in Algorithm 7
and illustrated in Figure 3.8.
One may also wonder why we add ϵ to zt−1 in Step (SDDPM3). Randomness at each
step prevents mode collapse or a reduction in the overall probability mass of the generative
process, analogous to sampling in language models.
At each step, we do not greedily
select the highest-probability token, even though the overall objective is to maximize the
probability of the complete sequence. In this context, beam search often performs better
than naive random sampling. Nevertheless, at the final step, we use the expectation µθ
1(z1)
instead of a sampled value from the corresponding distribution. This choice is consistent
with the first term of our ELBO (3.32). In practice, all regression models ultimately output
expected values rather than stochastic samples.

3.2.3 Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs)
We have observed that diffusion models can be computationally demanding, as they se-
quentially reverse a noise process that may involve hundreds or even thousands of steps.
Song et al. (2020a) introduced a related framework called denoising diffusion implicit mod-
els (DDIMs), which relaxes the Markovian assumption of the noise process while preserving
the same training objective; see Section 3.3. This approach delivers a one or two order-of-
magnitude speedup during sampling without compromising the quality of generated sam-
ples.
Generative 
model
Latent distribution
Data distribution
(a) DDPM.
Decoder
Generative 
model
Latent distribution
Data distribution
Latent distribution
(b) LDM.
Figure 3.9: Conceptual illustration of the difference between standard DDPMs and LDMs.
LDMs represent a computationally efficient variant of DDPMs. Instead of performing diffu-
sion directly on high-resolution data, LDMs conduct diffusion on compressed latent tokens
and use a decoder to reconstruct the final output, enabling scalable generation of high-
fidelity samples compared to conventional DDPMs.
A different strategy to mitigate the high computational cost of applying diffusion models
directly in high-resolution data space is known as latent diffusion models (LDMs) (Rombach
et al., 2022). Standard DDPMs carry out the full forward noising and reverse denoising
processes directly on the high-dimensional raw data distribution, operating in the pixel
space of the target signal.
Although this formulation produces high-fidelity samples, it
incurs significant computational overhead, as the model must learn denoising transitions
across the full dimensionality of the input data. High-resolution data exhibit extremely
large dimensionality, meaning hundreds of iterative diffusion steps require substantial GPU
memory and heavy computation. This makes high-quality, high-resolution generation slow
and resource-intensive, limiting deployment on standard hardware.


118
3.2. DENOISING DIFFUSION PROBABILISTIC MODELS (DDPMS)
In contrast, LDMs adopt a two-stage paradigm: first, a learned encoder (such as the vari-
ational autoencoder, or VAE, described in Section 2.2) maps the data into a low-dimensional
latent space that retains only the most semantically relevant information. Critically, this
latent representation—also referred to as a latent code, embedding, token, or latent vector—
captures the essential semantic and structural information of the input while discarding re-
dundant noise and fine-grained details. The diffusion process is then applied exclusively to
these compact latent representations. The final signal is reconstructed from the generated
latents using a corresponding decoder, or “detokenizer.” By restricting the computation-
ally expensive diffusion steps to a lower-dimensional manifold, LDMs achieve substantially
improved training and sampling efficiency while maintaining the generative quality of the
original framework, thus enabling scalable synthesis of high-resolution outputs. The funda-
mental difference between DDPMs and LDMs lies in the domain where the diffusion process
is defined. A comparison between DDPMs and LDMs is shown in Figure 3.9. Further details
on the architectures used within the LDM framework can be found in Section 7.2.
𝒙𝟎∈𝑅𝐷
input data
𝒛𝟎∈𝑅𝐾
encoded data
𝒙𝟎
’ ∈𝑅𝐷
decompressed data
ℰ(∙)
𝒟(∙)
(a) Encoding in a LDM.
𝒛𝟎
𝒟(∙)
𝒛𝟏

𝒙𝟎

(b) DDPM operates in the latent space in a LDM.
Figure 3.10: Illustration of latent diffusion models. Encoding the original data x0 into a
latent vector z0; and diffusion is applied to the latent vector in a LDM.
Specifically, LDMs move the entire diffusion process from high-resolution pixel space into
a compressed latent space. First, a pre-trained autoencoder (such as a variational autoen-
coder, or VAE) compresses raw data into compact low-dimensional latent representations:
z0 = E(x0). All forward noising and reverse denoising steps of diffusion are then performed
on these small latent vectors rather than full-resolution data. Intuitively, a well-trained
autoencoder can be seen as filtering out high-frequency or semantically irrelevant details,
allowing the generative model to focus on important, perceptually meaningful features. Fi-
nally, the decoded latent output z′
0 is mapped back to a high-quality data: x′
0 = D(z′
0).
This encoder (paired with its corresponding decoder), or VAE, can be a pre-trained model
(see, for example, Algorithm 3) with parameters frozen during the training of the latent
diffusion model, as illustrated in Figure 3.10(a). This compression drastically reduces com-
putational cost and memory consumption while preserving essential visual information. The
core diffusion logic remains unchanged; only the operating domain is shifted to latent space
for improved efficiency, as shown in Figure 3.10(b).

is optimal when x0 is deterministically fixed to a single point. These represent two extreme
choices corresponding to upper and lower bounds on the entropy of the reverse process
for data with coordinatewise unit variance (Sohl-Dickstein et al., 2015). In practice, the
two choices yield similar results (Ho et al., 2020). Given that βt and (σq
t )2 correspond to
opposite extremes, it is natural to ask why this choice has little effect on generated samples.
In Figure 3.11(a), we show that βt and (σq
t )2 are nearly identical except near t = 0, where
the model handles imperceptible fine details. Furthermore, as the number of diffusion steps
increases, βt and (σq
t )2 remain close to each other over a larger portion of the diffusion
process. This suggests that, in the limit of infinitely many diffusion steps, the selection of
(σθ
t )2 may have no meaningful impact on sample quality. In other words, as more diffusion
steps are added, the model mean µθ
t (zt) dominates the learned distribution far more than
the variance term (σθ
t )2ID.
Although the above reasoning supports fixing (σθ
t )2 as a reasonable choice for sample
quality, it does not address log-likelihood. In fact, Nichol and Dhariwal (2021) showed that
the first few steps of the diffusion process contribute most heavily to the evidence lower-
bound. It is therefore plausible that log-likelihood can be improved by selecting a more
appropriate form for (σθ
t )2. To achieve this, we learn (σθ
t )2 by interpolating between the
two extremes βt and (σq
t )2. Nichol and Dhariwal (2021) found that interpolation in the log
domain performs well:

In the original DDPM paper Ho et al. (2020), which uses a total of T = 1000 steps, the
noise schedule is defined to be linear:

αt = cos2

αt decreases linearly in the middle of the diffusion process, while remaining nearly constant
near the boundaries t = 0 and t = T, thus avoiding abrupt variations in noise magnitude. By
comparison, αt under the linear schedule decays toward zero much more rapidly, discarding
signal information faster than necessary. This explains why the forward noising process
becomes excessively noisy at low resolutions (e.g., for images of size 64 × 64 or 32 × 32) and
therefore contributes little to final sample quality.
3.2.6 Score-Based Interpretation of DDPMs
The denoising diffusion models presented in this chapter are closely connected to another
family of deep generative models developed largely independently, which are built upon
score matching (Hyv¨arinen and Dayan, 2005; Song and Ermon, 2019).
This subsection
focuses exclusively on the equivalence between DDPMs and score matching. A more general
score-matching framework grounded in stochastic differential equations is introduced in
Section 5.2.
We first introduce the preliminary background of Tweedie’s formula.
In statistics,
unknown parameters of probability distributions are commonly estimated from observed
samples via approaches such as maximum likelihood estimation and Bayesian estimation.
Tweedie’s formula serves as a dedicated method for estimating the mean parameter of dis-
tributions belonging to the exponential family 6. As Gaussian distributions fall into the
exponential family, Tweedie’s formula is applicable to estimating their mean parameters.
We omit the mathematical proof of Tweedie’s formula and focus solely on its practical
application herein.
For a multivariate Gaussian random variable y ∼N(y | µy, Σy), Tweedie’s formula is
defined as:
(Tweedie’s formula)
E[µy | y] = y + Σy ∇y ln p(y)
|
{z
}
score
,
(3.59)
where the left-hand side E[µy | y] denotes the conditional expectation of µy given the
observed sample y, which intuitively corresponds to estimating the mean parameter µy
from the sample y. Here, ln p(y) is the log-likelihood of the observed sample, and sp(y) ≜
6. See, for example, Lu (2021b) for more details.

∇y ln p(y) denotes the gradient of the sample log-likelihood, termed the Stein score, or
simply the score. 7
We now return to the forward transition kernel defined in (3.10). The forward diffusion

Remark 3.1.
There are two main approaches to representing the score function sθ(z)
using a deep neural network. Each element si of s corresponds to an element zi of z, so
the first method uses a network with an equal number of output and input dimensions.
However, the score function is defined as the gradient of a scalar function (the log-
probability density), which belongs to a more constrained function class. An alternative
approach is to use a network with a single scalar output ψ(z) and then compute ∇zψ(z)
via automatic differentiation. This second method requires two backpropagation passes
and is thus computationally more costly. For this reason, most practical applications
adopt the first approach.
We further establish the intrinsic connection between the score gradient and the diffusion
noise.
By combining (3.62) and the diffusion kernel formulation (3.12), we obtain the
following equivalence:
x0 = zt + (1 −αt)∇ln p(zt)
√αt
= zt −√1 −αtϵt
√αt
(3.66)
=⇒
∇ln p(zt) = −
1
√1 −αt
ϵt.
(3.67)
The gradient direction corresponds to the direction of function maximization: ∇ln p(zt)
points toward increasing log-likelihood (and thus higher probability density) of p(zt), i.e.,
an ascent direction (see Figure 3.12 illustrative example). The above result reveals that the
true score gradient is exactly the negative of the diffusion noise, meaning the two vectors
point in opposite directions. This opposing relationship between score gradient and injected
noise offers clear intuitive interpretability for the diffusion generation process: the ascent
direction corresponds to reversing the added noise.

3.2.7 Final Remarks: Three Equivalent Views of Diffusion Models
To this point, we have presented three distinct formulations for diffusion models: (i) directly
predicting the initial sample; (ii) predicting noise; and (iii) predicting the score. While these
three formulations are theoretically equivalent, they differ considerably in terms of learning
difficulty and model flexibility. Below, we summarize and compare these three approaches
for clarity and review.
We provide a compact review of the DDPM mechanism. In DDPM, real-world data (e.g.,
images) is modeled as a random variable x0, with its underlying data distribution denoted
as pdata(x0). Since the true form of pdata(x0) is intractable and unknown, direct sampling
from this exact distribution is infeasible. Fortunately, abundant empirical observations of
x0 are available. We can therefore approximate pdata(x0) using these observed samples and
generate new data from the approximated distribution.
The core principle of DDPM is to construct a Markov chain that gradually injects Gaus-
sian random noise into x0, ultimately converting the original structured data into pure stan-
dard Gaussian noise. This noise-injection procedure is defined as the forward (or noising)
process. The corresponding reverse procedure is a progressive denoising process, which esti-
mates the denoising transition distribution p(zt−1 | zt) at each timestep. New data samples
are generated by iteratively denoising pure Gaussian noise zT sampled from the standard
normal distribution, where T denotes the total number of diffusion timesteps—namely,
the number of noise injection steps in the forward diffusion process and the corresponding
number of denoising steps in the reverse process (illustrated in Figure 3.3).
The joint distribution of the entire diffusion process is formulated as p(x0, z1:T ). Fol-
lowing the probability chain rule, the forward diffusion process decomposes the joint distri-
bution via the diffusion kernel in (3.9):
p(x0, z1:T ) = pdata(x0)q(z1 | x0)
T
Y

q(zt | zt−1).
(3.68)
Although the analytical form of pdata(x0) remains unknown, empirical observations of x0 are
accessible. The forward transition q(zt | zt−1) follows a conditional Gaussian distribution
with the probability density function specified in (3.8) and we recall below:
q(zt | zt−1) = N

sition kernel characterizes the Gaussian noise injection operation that transforms zt−1 to zt.

3.2. DENOISING DIFFUSION PROBABILISTIC MODELS (DDPMS)
Through iterative application of this kernel, the structured original data x0 is progressively
corrupted into pure Gaussian noise zT .
However, iterative step-wise noising computation is computationally inefficient. To ad-
dress this issue, we leverage a mathematical property of conditional Gaussian distributions
to directly compute the latent state zt at any arbitrary timestep from the original data
x0 in a single step. This one-step mapping is formalized by the conditional distribution
q(zt | x0) from (3.10):

defines the denoising process.
The primary optimization objective is to approximate the intractable true reverse tran-
sition q(zt−1 | zt) via a parameterized model pθ(zt−1 | zt) with learnable parameters θ.

diffusion models introduced earlier and we will conclude below.

likelihood of observed real data ln pθ(x0). Notably, only x0 is observable among all T + 1
random variables in the diffusion graphical model. Thus, we marginalize the joint distribu-
tion pθ(x0, z1:T ) over all latent variables to obtain the marginal data distribution pθ(x0):

Here, the model learns to predict the score (log-likelihood gradient) ∇ln p(zt). Relative to
noise prediction, score-based learning offers the key advantage that it enables the use of
score-based sampling algorithms during reverse-process data generation. A wide variety of
score-based sampling methods exist, which greatly enhances overall algorithmic flexibility.
3.3. Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models (DDIMs)
In DDPMs, the data generation process is formulated as the reverse of a Markovian diffu-
sion process. At each step of reverse sampling, the model predicts the injected noise (see
Section 3.2.1). However, Song et al. (2020a) demonstrated that diffusion processes are not
strictly required to follow Markovian assumptions, leading to the proposal of denoising dif-
fusion implicit models (DDIMs). Subsequent studies on score-based diffusion models and
stochastic differential equation (SDE)-based diffusion theories have independently validated
this conclusion (see Section 5.2).
3.3.1 Non-Markovian Forward Process
The DDPM training loss formulated in (3.53) or (3.75) indicates that model training relies
on single-timestep noised sample construction. Specifically, given a clean data x0, a noise
vector ϵt ∼N(0, ID) is sampled to generate the noised feature zt = √αtx0 + √1 −αtϵt
(see (3.10)). This construction only utilizes the marginal distribution q(zt | x0), which
characterizes the direct mapping between a clean data and its noisy counterpart at a single
diffusion level t. Intermediate latent states along the Markov chain (z1, z2, . . . , zt−1) are
never involved in training. The network is exclusively trained on paired data consisting of
original clean data and their corresponding noised versions at arbitrary timesteps t, with
the sole objective of noise prediction.
Accordingly, the trained diffusion network acts as a standalone function that takes a
marginal sample from q(zt | x0) and a timestep index t as inputs to estimate the injected
noise (or equivalently, to recover the original data x0 or compute the score function). Crit-
ically, the training objective neither incorporates nor depends on the Markovian nature of
the forward diffusion chain. Although the Markovian forward process serves as the theo-
retical basis for loss derivation, it leaves no inherent constraint or structural fingerprint on


127
CHAPTER 3.
DIFFUSION MODELS
the final loss formulation. This implies the training objective does not uniquely necessitate
the Markovian forward process assumed by standard DDPMs.
To address this, DDIM constructs a family of non-Markovian forward diffusion processes
that exactly preserve the marginal distributions of DDPMs.
Due to identical marginal
constraints, pre-trained DDPM models can be directly adopted for DDIM sampling without
additional fine-tuning or retraining.
▶Defining DDIMs.
Based on the above analysis, eliminating the Markovian assumption
while preserving full consistency with standard DDPMs (i.e., retaining the original DDPM
training objective) only requires invariance of the conditional distributions q(zt | x0). We
next formalize the construction of the non-Markovian forward diffusion model for DDIMs.
We reparameterize the joint distribution q(z1:T | x0) by introducing a free hyperparame-
ter σ2, which controls the variance of the conditional transition distribution qσ(zt−1 | zt, x0).
Detailed analysis of σ2 is provided in subsequent discussions. The joint distribution of the
non-Markovian forward process is defined as:
qσ(z1:T | x0) ≜qσ(zT | x0)
T
Y
t=2
qσ(zt−1 | zt, x0),
(3.82)
where T denotes the total number of diffusion steps. We enforce the terminal marginal
distribution to be identical to that of standard DDPMs, as specified in (3.10) or (3.70a):
(Terminal marginal)
qσ(zT | x0) = N (zT | √αT x0, (1 −αT )ID) .
(3.83)
For all timesteps t > 1, DDIM directly defines the backward transition kernel qσ(zt−1 |
zt, x0) without inverting a Markovian diffusion chain, as follows (cf. (3.17) in DDPMs):
(Reverse kernel)

holds, if:

Unlike the strictly Markovian forward process adopted in conventional DPMs and DDPMs
(3.4), the DDIM forward process is non-Markovian, as each latent state zt depends on both
the preceding state zt−1 and the original clean data x0 (during sampling, x0 is replaced
by the model’s predicted estimate bx0). This breaks the single-step conditional indepen-
dence constraint inherent to Markovian diffusion chains. The hyperparameter σt governs
the stochasticity of the forward diffusion process. In the limiting case where σt →0, the
diffusion process becomes fully deterministic: given x0 and any intermediate state zt, all
previous latent states zt−1 are uniquely determined with no randomness.
▶Reverse process and objective function.
We next define a trainable generative
process pθ(x0, z1:T ), in which each transition pθ(zt−1 | zt) incorporates the structural for-
mulation of the conditional distribution qσ(zt−1 | zt, x0). Consistent with standard DPMs
and DDPMs, our goal is to approximate the true data-dependent conditional distribution
q(zt−1 | zt, x0) (see Sections 3.1.2 and 3.2.1). Intuitively, given a noisy latent sample zt,
the model first estimates the corresponding clean data x0, and then generates the previous
latent state zt−1 via the predefined reverse conditional distribution qσ(zt−1 | zt, x0).
For a clean data x0 ∼pdata(x0) sampled from the real data distribution and Gaussian
noise ϵt ∼N(0, ID), the noisy latent zt can be constructed following either (3.10b) or
(3.70b). The standard DDPM model ϵθ

timestep-wise marginal distributions rather than chain-specific transitional properties, the
learned network function remains fully compatible. This explains the zero-retraining prop-
erty of DDIM: DDIM does not require model fine-tuning, as it implements a novel sampling


130
3.3.
DENOISING DIFFUSION IMPLICIT MODELS (DDIMS)
algorithm built upon the pre-trained DDPM inference function. Different configurations
of σt yield distinct generative trajectories while reusing the fixed DDPM noise-prediction
model ϵθ
t , eliminating the need for parameter retraining.
In summary, the non-Markovian diffusion construction delivers two core advantages.
First, the sequence {σt} constitutes a set of free hyperparameters.
Different σt choices
produce distinct reverse diffusion processes while preserving identical marginal distributions
to DDPMs. A specific σt configuration exactly recovers the stochastic sampling scheme of
vanilla DDPMs, whereas setting σt = 0 eliminates stochastic noise injection and yields a
fully deterministic sampling process.
Second, the DDIM formulation is defined based on independent marginal distributions
at individual timesteps, rather than adjacent transitional dependencies in Markov chains.
This allows sampling to be performed over arbitrary increasing timestep subsequences (e.g.,
0, 50, 100, . . . , 1000), enabling direct sampling along heavily shortened diffusion schedules.
All intermediate updates remain marginally consistent with the original diffusion process,
which suppoAmenabilityrts sampling speedups of one to two orders of magnitude without
retraining. Unlike rigid Markovian diffusion chains that require consecutive timestep tran-
sitions, the non-Markovian DDIM framework removes adjacent-step constraints and only
enforces marginal distribution consistency.
3.3.2 Accelerated Sampling
The non-Markovian nature of DDIM diffusion enables flexible acceleration of the sampling
procedure. As discussed above, the DDPM training objective depends exclusively on per-
timestep marginal distributions and is agnostic to the chain-specific transition order of the
forward process. Consequently, pre-trained DDPM models can be generalized to forward
processes with fewer diffusion steps, yielding faster generative sampling pipelines without
additional model training or fine-tuning.
Instead of constructing the forward diffusion process over the full sequence of latent
variables z1:T , we consider a reduced process defined on a strictly increasing timestep sub-
sequence S = {S1, S2, . . . , SS} sampled from the full range [1, 2, . . . , T], where S ≪T denotes
the truncated sequence length. We enforce each subsampled latent state to preserve the
exact marginal distribution of the original DDPM process:
q(zSi | x0) = N
 √αSix0, (1 −αSi)ID

.
The generative sampling procedure is then conducted over the reversed subsequence of se-
lected timesteps, defined as the sampling trajectory. By adopting a substantially shortened
sampling trajectory, we significantly reduce the total number of iterative update steps,
thereby improving the computational efficiency of image generation.
Furthermore, the reverse diffusion update can be reinterpreted from a score-based per-
spective. Following the theoretical derivation in Section 3.2.6, the log-likelihood gradient of
the latent variable zt is linearly correlated with the model’s predicted noise:
∇ln p(zt) = −

This

sentially performs iterative updates along the log-likelihood ascent direction of the latent
samples, which is analogous to parameter optimization via gradient descent. This key in-
sight enables the integration of advanced optimization techniques into diffusion sampling,
including adaptive learning rate strategies and mainstream optimization algorithms such as
Adam (Kingma and Ba, 2014), to further optimize sampling performance and convergence.
3.4. Guidance: Condition on a Prompt
Thus far, we have framed diffusion models as tools for approximating the unguided (uncon-
ditional) data density pθ(x) ≈pdata(x), which is trained on samples X = {x1, x2, . . . , xN}
independently drawn from the underlying true data distribution pdata(x). Once training
is complete, the fitted model can generate new synthetic data (e.g., synthetic images) by
sampling from this learned distribution pθ(x). Both DDPM and DDIM adhere to the same
generative paradigm for data synthesis: they take randomly sampled Gaussian noise as
input and iteratively refine this noisy input into coherent, realistic outputs such as natu-
ral images. Since each denoising step constitutes a stochastic transformation, independent
sampling runs produce distinct data, endowing generated samples with strong diversity.
However, this stochasticity introduces a key limitation: the generation process lacks con-
trollability, meaning the sampling pipeline cannot be systematically directed to produce
data content aligned with custom target objectives.
In most practical applications, by contrast, sampling is performed from a guided (con-
ditional) distribution p(x | y) where the guidance variable y may correspond to a class
label, textual description, or other desired attribute. This conditional formulation under-
pins numerous downstream applications, including text-guided diffusion generation, image
super-resolution, and image inpainting, among others. Representative use cases are detailed
below:
Text-guided diffusion models. These models integrate techniques from large language
models to accept general text sequences—referred to as prompts—as guidance input,
rather than restricting guidance to predefined class labels. Text prompts modulate
the denoising process through two core mechanisms: first, by concatenating latent


132
3.4. GUIDANCE: CONDITION ON A PROMPT
representations extracted from transformer-based language models (Vaswani et al.,
2017) with the input to the denoising network; second, by enabling cross-attention
layers within the denoising network to attend explicitly to text token sequences. See
Chapter 7 for more details.
Image super-resolution. Guided diffusion models also excel at image super-resolution,
which maps low-resolution inputs to corresponding high-resolution counterparts. This
task is inherently an ill-posed inverse problem, as multiple valid high-resolution im-
ages can correspond to a single low-resolution input. Diffusion-based super-resolution
achieves high-fidelity upsampling by denoising high-resolution Gaussian samples con-
ditioned on low-resolution guidance images (Saharia et al., 2022b).
Cascaded diffusion pipelines.
Cascaded diffusion pipelines further enhance resolu-
tion capabilities, enabling ultra-high-resolution synthesis (e.g., progressive upsampling
from 64 × 64 to 256 × 256, then to 1024 × 1024) (Ho et al., 2022). Each stage typ-
ically employs a U-Net architecture (Ronneberger et al., 2015), where each U-Net is
conditioned on the fully denoised output of the preceding stage.
This cascaded strategy is also applicable to standard image-generation diffusion mod-
els. In this framework, initial denoising occurs at a lower resolution, and a dedicated
upsampling network (which may additionally accept text prompts) subsequently up-
samples the low-resolution output to produce high-resolution final results (Nichol
et al., 2021; Saharia et al., 2022a). This approach serves as an alternative to the latent
diffusion models (LDMs) introduced in Section 3.2.3 and substantially reduces com-
putational overhead. Because denoising requires hundreds of forward passes through
the network, operating on low-resolution image spaces avoids the high costs of direct
high-dimensional denoising. Notably, these methods still operate directly in the pixel
image space rather than latent space.
To address the limited controllability of unconditional diffusion models, extensive prior
work has developed effective strategies for steering the data generation process. The core
intuition is to inject auxiliary guidance information into the diffusion sampling process to
regulate model behavior. We denote this guidance information as y, which can take the
form of text prompts, reference images, or categorical class labels. With the introduction
of y, the model characterizes a conditional diffusion distribution:
p(z1:T | y, x0).
Remark 3.3 (Terminology for conditional and guided).
To avoid notation and
terminology conflicts arising from the term “conditional”—which conventionally refers to
conditioning on the data or latent distribution (e.g., the reverse approximation pθ(zt−1 |
zt)), we strictly reserve the term “guided” exclusively for conditioning on external guid-
ance variables y, such as text prompts.
▶Forward process with guidance.
We now analyze how the guidance variable y
affects the forward and reverse diffusion processes. We define the hat notation bq to denote
probability distributions associated with the guided diffusion model conditioned on y, while
the unhatted notation q denotes distributions of the original (unguided) diffusion model. We
first consider a single diffusion timestep t. Diffusion models form Markov chains, meaning
the latent state zt depends only on the previous state zt−1. Consequently, the forward


133

DIFFUSION MODELS
noising transition at each timestep is independent of external guidance y, yielding the key
identity:
(A5)

(3.97)

of y, as derived below:

unguided diffusion models.
▶Reverse process with guidance.
We next examine the effect of y on the reverse
generative sampling process, where guidance is intentionally introduced to control synthesis
outcomes. For the original unguided diffusion model, the joint distribution of the reverse
Markov chain is recalled from (3.23):
pθ(x0, z1:T ) = p(zT )

The entire controllability of guided diffusion generation is exclusively embedded within the
reverse denoising process. By conditioning the reverse transition distributions on y, the
model reshapes the trajectory of latent state restoration to align with external semantic
or structural constraints, while preserving the diversity inherent to stochastic diffusion
sampling.
This core property explains why naive conditional injection—feeding y as a
network input—can enable conditional generation in principle.


134
3.4. GUIDANCE: CONDITION ON A PROMPT
▶Simple guidance approaches.
Collectively, these results clarify the essential working
mechanism of guided diffusion models: conditional generation does not alter data corrup-
tion, but steers data reconstruction. This insight lays the theoretical foundation for ad-
vanced guidance strategies, which are designed to amplify guidance signal influence during
reverse sampling, balance conditional fidelity and sample diversity, and resolve the under-
constrained optimization problem of naive conditional diffusion training.
The most straightforward strategy for implementing conditional guidance is to feed y as
an additional network input and train the model on paired training data {xn, yn}N
n=1. As
summarized in Section 3.2.7, diffusion probabilistic models admit three equivalent training
interpretations:
(i) Directly predict the original data x0: bxθ
t (zt) ≃x0.
(ii) Predict the noise data: ϵθ
t (zt) ≃ϵt.
(iii) Predict the score (gradient): sθ
t (zt) ≃∇zt ln p(zt).
In the simplest guided formulation, the guidance variable y is appended to the network
input in the same manner as the timestep embedding t. The three learning objectives are
modified to accept conditioning:
(i) Directly predict the original data x0: bxθ
t (zt | y) ≃x0.
(ii) Predict the noise data: ϵθ
t (zt | y) ≃ϵt.
(iii) Predict the score (gradient): sθ
t (zt | y) ≃∇zt ln p(zt).
Although this naive conditioning strategy is functional, it suffers from a critical limitation:
the network may assign insufficient weight to the guidance signal or overlook it entirely
during optimization. This motivates explicit mechanisms to modulate guidance strength,
enabling a controllable trade-off between alignment to the guidance condition and sample
diversity.
Such explicit alignment constraints are formally termed guidance techniques.
Broadly speaking, guidance methods fall into two primary categories: those relying on an
external classifier model and those that do not.
3.4.1 Classifier Guidance
Dhariwal and Nichol (2021) proposed a framework for guiding image generation using image
class labels, referred to as classifier guidance. This technique substantially improved the
quality of images synthesized by diffusion models, outperforming GANs in FID metrics (see
Problem 7.2). While the paper introduced several enhancements, including modifications
to the U-Net architecture (Ronneberger et al., 2015), we focus exclusively on the guidance
mechanism in this work.
As noted earlier, the predictive model used in the denoising step of a diffusion model
can be interpreted from three perspectives; see Section 3.2.7. One interpretation is that
the parameterized neural network predicts the log-gradient of zt, also known as the score,
which is expressed as:
sθ
t (zt) ≃∇zt ln p(zt).
When guidance information y is introduced, the original score ∇zt ln p(zt) becomes ∇zt ln p(zt |
y), defined as the score of the guided (diffusion) model, evaluated at arbitrary noise levels
t. Applying Bayes’ theorem (1.10), the score function of the guided diffusion model can be


135
CHAPTER 3.
DIFFUSION MODELS
decomposed as follows:
∇zt ln p(zt | y)
|

= ∇zt ln
p(zt)p(y | zt)


