Adversarial gradient
.
(3.101)
This expansion yields two distinct terms. The first term on the right-hand side of (3.101)
is the standard unguided score function, while the second term—called the adversarial
gradient—steers the denoising process toward maximizing the probability of the given la-
bel/guidance under the classifier model (Song et al., 2020c; Dhariwal and Nichol, 2021).
Thus, the guided score ∇zt ln p(zt | y) can be interpreted as the unguided score ∇zt ln p(zt)
combined with an adversarial gradient derived from the classifier p(y | zt).
The input to the classifier p(y | zt) is not the clean data x0, but the noisy latent variable
zt at the corresponding timestep t. This classifier must be trained independently prior to
training the guided diffusion model. Specifically, during classifier training, zt generated
via the forward noising process of the diffusion model (see (3.10)) serves as the input. In
classifier guidance, the unguided diffusion model score is learned as previously derived,
together with a classifier that accepts arbitrary noisy zt and predicts the guidance label y.
The overall pipeline consists of three steps:
(i) Learn the unguided diffusion model as derived earlier; see (3.65).
(ii) Pre-train a noise-conditioned classifier p(y | zt), where t indexes different timesteps
(i.e., different noise levels).
(iii) During sampling at each timestep t of the original unguided diffusion model:
(a). Compute the unguided score predicted by the diffusion model: sθ
t (zt).
(b). Obtain the noisy latent zt via forward process (3.10), then evaluate the adver-
sarial gradient p(y | zt): ∇zt ln p(y | zt).
(c). Compute the guided score: sθ
t (zt, y) = sθ
t (zt) + ∇zt ln p(y | zt).
(d). Continue with the standard denoising procedure.
The adversarial gradient ∇zt ln p(y | zt) from the noise classifier modulates the sampling
process, directing denoising toward the target class y and producing data that closely align
with the specified label. For instance, if y = “horse”, the model generates horse images
with improved realism. However, although classifier guidance enhances visual fidelity, it
noticeably reduces sample diversity.
To enable more flexible control over the generation direction, the original work intro-
duces a hyperparameter λ as a weighting coefficient. This scalar adjusts the strength of
class guidance, yielding the scaled guided diffusion score:
g
∇zt ln p(zt | y) = ∇zt ln p(zt) + λ∇zt ln p(y | zt),
(3.102)
where λ ≥0 is known as the guidance scale (typically set to λ > 1). Intuitively, when λ = 0,
the guided model disregards label information entirely. As λ increases, the model prioritizes
adherence to the guidance signal, which comes at the expense of sample diversity, as it tends


136
3.4. GUIDANCE: CONDITION ON A PROMPT
to produce only samples that easily reconstruct the given label even under significant noise.
Notably, this formulation is heuristic: for λ̸ = 1, we have g
∇zt ln p(zt | y)̸ = ∇zt ln p(zt | y),
meaning it does not represent the “true” score of the guided diffusion model.
Subsequent work by Liu et al. (2023) generalized the notion of a “classifier” to support
image-to-image and text-to-image generation. While retaining the core mathematical struc-
ture, this approach replaces the dedicated classifier with a differentiable semantic scoring
function that is not trained on noisy inputs and is not restricted to discrete class labels.
A key limitation of classifier guidance is its dependence on a separately trained classifier
p(y | zt). Since most off-the-shelf pre-trained classification models are not optimized for
highly noisy inputs, such a classifier must be trained from scratch alongside the diffusion
model. Furthermore, the classifier’s label space is inherently finite and cannot cover all
possible concepts, making it poorly suited for out-of-distribution or novel categories. For
these reasons, we next consider an alternative framework that eliminates the need for an
independent classifier.

3.4.2 Classifier-Free Guidance
Shortly after the classifier guidance work, the Google Brain team introduced classifier-free
diffusion guidance (CFG) (Ho and Salimans, 2022).
This mechanism was subsequently
adopted by several prominent models, including OpenAI’s GLIDE andDALL-E 2 (Nichol
et al., 2021; Ramesh et al., 2022), Google’s Imagen (Saharia et al., 2022a), and StabilityAI’s
Stable Diffusion series (Rombach et al., 2022).
Classifier-free guidance can be derived
through a minor modification of classifier guidance. Rearranging (3.101), we obtain
∇zt ln p(y | zt) = ∇zt ln p(zt | y) −∇zt ln p(zt).

Substituting this expression into (3.102) yields the scaled score of the guided model in the
form
g
∇zt ln p(zt | y) = ∇zt ln p(zt) + λ (∇zt ln p(zt | y) −∇zt ln p(zt))
= ∇zt ln p(zt) + λ∇zt ln p(zt | y) −λ∇zt ln p(zt)
= λ∇zt ln p(zt | y)

(3.103)
Once again, λ controls the degree to which the trained guided model prioritizes the guidance
information.
When λ = 0, the guided model fully disregards the guidance signal and
reduces to an unguided diffusion model. When λ = 1, the model recovers the standard
guided distribution.
When λ > 1, the diffusion model not only emphasizes the guided
score function but also actively shifts away from the unguided score. In other words, it
lowers the probability of generating unguided samples in favor of those that strictly follow
the guidance signal. This again reduces sample diversity, but improves alignment between
generated samples and the provided guidance information.
Furthermore, we avoid training separate networks to model p(zt | y) and p(zt) by
training a single unified guided model.
During training, the guidance variable y is
randomly set to a null value (e.g., y = ∅) with a fixed probability γ, typically in the range
of 10–20%. In this setup, p(zt) is approximated by p(zt | y = ∅). This process is analogous
to dropout, where guidance inputs are collectively disabled for a random subset of training
examples. Classifier-free guidance is conceptually elegant: it provides finer control over
guided generation while requiring only a single diffusion model to be trained.
We thus
define our CFG score-based DDPM loss function as
J (θ) = E♢

The training procedure for the CFG-enabled score-based DDPM is outlined in Algorithm 9.
Alternative formulations can be trained using an analogous approach.
After training, the score function in (3.103) is used to enforce strong weighting of the
guidance signal. In practice, classifier-free guidance produces significantly higher-quality
outputs than classifier guidance (Nichol et al., 2021; Saharia et al., 2022a). This improve-
ment arises because a classifier p(y | zt) may ignore most components of the input zt as
long as it accurately predicts y. In contrast, classifier-free guidance relies directly on the
guided density p(zt | y), which must assign high probability to the full structure of zt.

141
CHAPTER 4. FLOW-BASED MODELS
I
In the rapidly advancing field of deep generative modeling, flow-based models have
emerged as a flexible, powerful framework capable of modeling complex probabil-
ity distributions via invertible transformations. Unlike generative adversarial net-
works (GANs), which depend on adversarial training, and variational autoencoders (VAEs)
or denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs), which are bounded by variational ap-
proximations, flow-based models directly parameterize probability densities through a series
of invertible mappings termed “flows.” This unique mechanism enables exact unbiased likeli-
hood estimation, efficient sampling, and interpretable latent space representations. Over the
past decade, flow-based models have evolved from basic discrete transformations to sophis-
ticated continuous dynamical systems. Major variants include coupling flows, autoregressive
flows, continuous flows, and the recently proposed flow matching paradigm. Each variant
addresses distinct challenges in generative modeling—ranging from high-dimensional data
processing to training complexity reduction—collectively advancing the state-of-the-art in
density estimation, image synthesis, natural language processing, and broader generative
tasks.
At their core, all flow-based models share a fundamental principle: by decomposing a
complex target distribution into a series of invertible, differentiable transformations applied
to a simple base distribution (e.g., a Gaussian), they allow for straightforward computation
of the target density using the change of variables theorem (see Figure 4.1 for a visual
illustration). This principle not only ensures exact likelihood estimation—a critical advan-
tage for tasks like density estimation and out-of-distribution detection—but also enables
controllable generation by manipulating the latent space (For example, base samples follow
a known distribution that is easy to sample, and target samples are given to us in terms
of a dataset of finite size. Depending on the application, target samples may constitute
images, videos, audio segments, or other types of high-dimensional, richly structured data.
The goal is then to generate random samples of interest). This objective aligns closely with
the central focus of this book (see Figure 1.2).
However, the design of these invertible
transformations has undergone significant evolution, leading to the diversity of flow types
we see today.
Discrete flow variants, such as coupling flow and autoregressive flow, laid the ground-
work for flow-based modeling by introducing structured invertible mappings that balance
expressiveness and computational efficiency. Coupling flow, popularized by models like Re-
alNVP, partitions the input into disjoint subsets and transforms one subset conditional on
the other, leveraging the flexibility of conditional neural networks while maintaining invert-
ibility. Autoregressive flow, by contrast, constructs transformations in a sequential, autore-
gressive manner, where each dimension of the input is transformed conditional on previous
dimensions—offering strong expressiveness at the cost of sequential computation. These
discrete flows revolutionized generative modeling by demonstrating that invertible trans-
formations could scale to high-dimensional data, but they also faced limitations: coupling
flows often require careful partitioning strategies to avoid bottlenecks, while autoregressive
flows suffer from slow sampling due to their sequential nature.
The limitations of discrete flows paved the way for the development of continuous flow, a
paradigm that models transformations as continuous dynamical systems evolving over time.
Instead of a finite sequence of discrete steps, continuous flow represents the transformation
as a smooth, time-dependent flow governed by ordinary differential equations (ODEs), where
the input evolves continuously from the base distribution to the target distribution. This


142
continuous formulation eliminates the need for discrete partitioning or sequential processing,
enabling more flexible and efficient density estimation. However, training continuous flows
historically required computing the Jacobian determinant of the flow, a computationally
expensive step that limited their scalability.
Flow matching, a recent breakthrough in flow-based modeling, addressed this scalability
challenge by reimagining the training of continuous flows. Rather than directly optimiz-
ing the likelihood (and thus requiring Jacobian computations), flow matching trains the
velocity field of the continuous flow by matching it to a predefined reference vector field,
simplifying the training objective to a regression task. This innovation not only reduces
computational complexity but also unifies discrete and continuous flow paradigms, enabling
the development of hybrid models that combine the strengths of both. Today, flow matching
has become a cornerstone of modern flow-based modeling, driving advances in high-quality
generation, conditional modeling, and bidirectional transport between distributions—often
integrated with coupling mechanisms to form powerful coupled flow matching frameworks.

Figure 4.1: The flow-based models blueprint. Fig 4.1(a) The goal of a flow-based model is
to find a flow mapping that transforms samples x0 from a known base or noise distribution
pbase into samples x1 from an unknown target or data distribution pdata. This mapping
is typically required to be invertible and differentiable, which is a core characteristic of
flow-based models.
Fig 4.1(b) To achieve this transformation, two common approaches
are adopted: for traditional flow-based models (e.g., coupling flow, masked autoregressive
flow), a sequence of invertible sub-mappings is constructed to directly transform pbase to
pdata without a continuous probability path; for models involving continuous dynamics (e.g.,
flow-matching), a time-continuous probability path {pt}0≤t≤1 interpolating between pbase
and pdata is designed. Fig 4.1(c) During training, the specific learning objective varies by
subcategory: for traditional flow-based models (e.g., coupling flow, masked autoregressive
flow), the parameters of the invertible sub-mappings are optimized to maximize the log-
likelihood of the data, leveraging the change-of-variable theorem to compute the probability
density of the target distribution; for flow-matching models, regression is used to estimate
the vector field vt that generates the probability path {pt}0≤t≤1. Fig 4.1(d) To draw a novel
target sample x1 ∼pdata, the approach depends on the model type: for traditional flow-
based models, apply the learned invertible flow mapping to a sample x0 ∼pbase to obtain the
target sample x1, leveraging the invertibility of the mapping to ensure valid transformation
from the base distribution to the target distribution; for flow-matching models, integrate
the estimated vector field vbθ
t (xt) from t = 0 to t = 1, where x0 ∼pbase is a novel source
sample.
This chapter aims to provide a comprehensive overview of flow-based models, focusing
on the four key variants: coupling flow, autoregressive flow, continuous flow, and flow


143
CHAPTER 4. FLOW-BASED MODELS
matching. We will dissect their core principles, mathematical formulations, advantages,
and limitations, trace their evolutionary trajectory, and explore how they complement and
enhance one another.
Additionally, the chapter extends flow matching models to score
matching models via stochastic differential equations (SDEs).
4.1. Normalizing Flows
We now discuss normalizing flow models for training nonlinear latent variable models. These
models constrain the structure of neural networks such that the data likelihood can be com-
puted exactly without approximation, while retaining straightforward sampling from the
trained model. Like other generative models we have introduced previously, we first define
a base distribution pbase(z) over the latent variable z, alongside a nonlinear deep neural
network transformation x = Dθ(z) that maps the latent space to the data space. When
pbase(z) is a simple distribution such as a Gaussian, sampling is trivial: one draws a latent
sample ez ∼pbase(z) and feeds it through the neural network to produce a corresponding
data sample ex = D(ez; θ).
Evaluating the model’s data likelihood requires the data-space probability distribution,
which relies on the inverse network transformation z = Eθ(x), satisfying the identity z =
Eθ(Dθ(z)). For this inverse to be well-defined for all valid parameters θ, the transformation
functions Dθ(z) and Eθ(x) must be invertible (bijective). Bijectivity ensures a one-to-
one correspondence between data samples x and latent samples z.
We then apply the
change-of-variables formula to obtain the exact data density:
pθ(x) = pbase(Eθ(x))

1
Notably, we still refer to z as a latent variable despite the deterministic, bijective mapping.
To align with the conventional autoencoder formulation, we adopt the notations E and
D throughout our analysis. Although each data point x maps to a unique, deterministic
latent value z, eliminating posterior uncertainty over latent states, we retain conventional
latent-variable terminology to ensure consistency with the preceding context.
To rigorously formalize the change-of-variables likelihood derivation (4.1) in full de-
tail, we next introduce the formal definitions of diffeomorphisms and push-forward op-
erators, which serve as the mathematical foundation of flow-based generative modeling.
Definition 4.1 (Diffeomorphisms and push-forward maps).
Let Cr(Rm, Rn) de-
note the space of r-times continuously differentiable functions f : Rm →Rn, whose
1. The determinant is a scalar-valued function that maps a square matrix A ∈RD×D to a real num-
ber, denoted det(A) or |A|. Geometrically, it represents the signed volume scaling factor of the linear
transformation induced by A: it describes how much a unit volume in RD is stretched, compressed, or
reflected by the transformation. A nonzero determinant indicates the matrix is invertible (nonsingular),
while a zero determinant means the matrix is singular (non-invertible).

Cr diffeomorphism. A function D ∈Cr(Rn, Rn) is a Cr diffeomorphism if it is bijective,
and its inverse satisfies D−1 ∈Cr(Rn, Rn).

In this work, the diffeomorphic mapping Dθ(z) is parameterized by a specialized neural
network architecture, which we elaborate on in subsequent sections. A key constraint of
diffeomorphic mappings is that the latent space and data space must share identical di-
mensionality. This dimensional matching often results in large model architectures when
processing high-dimensional data such as natural images. Additionally, the computational
cost of evaluating matrix determinants constitutes a major bottleneck in flow-based models.
For a D×D Jacobian matrix, determinant computation incurs a time complexity of O(D3),
where D denotes the input dimension or the number of hidden units (Lu, 2021a). To allevi-
ate this computational overhead, we introduce structural constraints on the network design
to enable efficient Jacobian determinant evaluation.
Consider a training dataset X = {x1, x2, . . . , xN} consisting of N independent and
identically distributed data samples. Using the change-of-variables result derived above,

still consistent with the main focus of this book; see Figure 1.2.
To model complex, real-world data distributions with high flexibility, the transformation
x = Dθ(z) is implemented via deep neural networks. To guarantee global invertibility of the
overall transformation, we construct the network as a composition of individually invertible
layers. Consider a three-layer composite diffeomorphism as an illustrative example:

This additive structure enables stable and efficient computation of the log-determinant
term in the log-likelihood, avoiding the high cost of directly evaluating large Jacobian
determinants, and simplifying gradient-based optimization.
This generative modeling paradigm is termed normalizing flows, as the sequential com-
position of invertible mappings gradually transforms a simple base distribution into a com-
plex data distribution, analogous to the continuous flow of fluid density across a manifold
(Rezende and Mohamed, 2015; Kobyzev et al., 2020; Bishop and Bishop, 2023). Notably,
the inverse flow mapping projects the complex empirical data distribution back to a stan-
dardized, simple base distribution (most commonly a standard Gaussian distribution). In
the remainder of this section, we introduce the core theoretical and architectural concepts
of two dominant families of practical normalizing flows: coupling flows and autoregressive
flows.


146
4.1. NORMALIZING FLOWS
4.1.1 Coupling Flows
When constructing coupling-based flow frameworks, we aim to design a standalone invertible
functional layer that can be stacked sequentially to yield a highly versatile class of invertible
transformations.
We first consider an affine linear mapping between the observed data
vector x and the latent variable z, formulated as
x = γz + β.
(4.7)
This mapping admits a straightforward inverse transformation:
z = 1
γ (x −β).
(4.8)
Nevertheless, linear transformations exhibit closure under composition,such that cascading
multiple linear layers collapses into a single unified linear transformation. Furthermore,
any affine transformation applied to a Gaussian distribution produces another Gaussian
distribution (see Lemma 1.12). Consequently, stacking arbitrarily many linear layers cannot
generate non-Gaussian distributions, resulting in fundamentally limited expressive capacity.
This motivates a key research question: can we preserve the exact invertibility of linear
mappings while introducing additional structural flexibility to model complex, non-Gaussian
data distributions?
▶RealNVP.
A canonical solution to this problem is implemented by the real-valued
non-volume-preserving (RealNVP) normalizing flow architecture proposed in Dinh et al.
(2014). The core mechanism involves splitting the D-dimensional latent vector z into two
disjoint subvectors: z = [z1; z2], where z1 is d-dimensional and z2 spans the remaining
D −d dimensions. The observed data vector x is partitioned identically as x = [x1; x2],
with matching dimensionality for x1 (d-dimensional) and x2 ((D −d)-dimensional). The
first partition of the output is defined as a direct copy of the corresponding latent partition:
x1 = z1.
(4.9a)
The second partition undergoes an affine transformation whose scaling and shifting coeffi-
cients are dynamically modulated by nonlinear neural network functions of z1:
x2 = exp (γθ(z1)) ◦z2 + βθ(z1).
(4.9b)
Here, γθ(z1) and βθ(z1) denote real-valued outputs from parameterized neural networks
with parameters θ. The elementwise exponential operation guarantees positive scaling co-
efficients for numerical stability. The operator ◦represents the Hadamard (elementwise)
vector product, and the exponential function in (4.9b) is also applied elementwise across
vector entries. While the formulation unifies the network parameters under a single θ no-
tation, practical implementations may employ two independent parameterized subnetworks
or a single network with dual output branches.
By virtue of the nonlinear neural modulation, the resulting mapping from x1 to x2 pos-
sesses substantial expressive flexibility. Critically, the full transformation remains exactly
invertible. Given an observed sample x = [x1; x2], the latent variables can be recovered via
the inverse procedure:
z1 = x1.
(4.10a)


147
CHAPTER 4. FLOW-BASED MODELS

Following the recovery of z1, we compute the network-modulated coefficients γθ(z1) and
βθ(z1), then solve for z2 as:
z2 = exp (−γθ(z1)) ◦(x2 −βθ(z1)) .
(4.10b)
The complete coupling transformation pipeline is visualized in Figure 4.2, where network Γ1
outputs exp(γθ(z1)) and network B1 outputs the shift term βθ(z1). Notably, the individual
neural network mappings γθ(z1) and βθ(z1) are not required to be invertible—only the full
composite coupling transformation maintains strict invertibility.
▶Jacobian and determinant calculation.
We next examine the computation of the
Jacobian matrix and its determinant. Partitioning the Jacobian according to the split of z
and x yields the block-structured matrix:
J =

exp(−γi).
(4.11)
The top-left block represents the derivatives of z1 with respect to x1, which from (4.10a) is
simply the d×d identity matrix. The top-right block contains derivatives of z1 with respect
to x2, which are identically zero, again by (4.10a). The bottom-left block corresponds to
derivatives of z2 with respect to x1, which from (4.10b) yield complex expressions involving
the neural network mappings. Finally, the bottom-right block gives derivatives of z2 with
respect to x2, which form a diagonal matrix with entries equal to the elementwise expo-
nentials of −γθ(z1). The Jacobian in (4.11) is thus lower triangular, with all entries above
the main diagonal equal to zero. For such matrices, the determinant equals the product
of the diagonal entries 2, and is therefore independent of the intricate expressions in the
bottom-left block. As a result, the Jacobian determinant simplifies directly to the product
of the elements of exp(−γθ(z1)).
▶Stacking the structure.
A clear drawback of this construction is that z1 remains
unmodified by the transformation. This issue is readily addressed by introducing an ad-
ditional layer (or multiple layers) where the roles of z1 and z2 are swapped, as depicted
in Figure 4.3. This two-layer pattern can then be iterated repeatedly to construct highly
expressive generative models.

Figure 4.3: Conceptual illustration of the full RealNVP framework. By stacking two layers
structured as in Figure 4.2, we construct a more expressive yet fully invertible nonlinear
layer. Since each individual sublayer supports exact inversion and admits tractable Jacobian
computation, the combined two-layer architecture preserves both desirable properties.
▶Training and density transformation.
The overall training pipeline operates on
mini-batches of data, where each sample contributes to the log-likelihood via (4.6), where
the base latent distribution is usually chosen as the standard Gaussian distribution. The
inverse mapping z = E(x) is computed by chaining inverse transformations of the form
(4.10b). And the log Jacobian determinant is obtained by summing the log determinants
of each layer, where each term is itself a sum of components of the form −γi
θ(x), i =

4.1.2 Autoregressive Flows
A distinct family of normalizing flows can be derived from a fundamental probabilistic
property: any joint distribution over a set of random variables can be factorized into a
product of univariate conditional distributions.
We first define a fixed ordering for the
elements of the vector x (for example, the raster scan order for image data). With this
ordering, the joint distribution can be universally rewritten as follows:
p(x1, x2, . . . , xD) =

where x1:i−1 denotes the subsequence x1, . . . , xi−1. This modeling paradigm aligns closely
with the first class of generative models introduced in Chapter 1. As illustrated in Figure 1.1,
it addresses the fundamental generative question: Given partial observed data, what is the
subsequent content? This characteristic property gives rise to the name autoregressive flows.

Autoregressive flows and coupling flows share fundamental structural similarities. While
autoregressive flows deliver strong modeling expressiveness, their sequential ancestral sam-
pling induces a computational cost that scales linearly with data dimensionality D. By
contrast, coupling flows can be interpreted as a constrained subclass of autoregressive flows.
These models trade full autoregressive generality for better computational efficiency by par-
titioning variables into only two groups, rather than D individual sequential components.
4.2. Flow Matching
In prior discussions, we formalized generative modeling as the process of sampling from
the data distribution pdata, and established the core objective of generative model devel-
opment: designing an algorithm capable of producing samples x ∼pdata.
This section
elaborates on the construction of generative models via the simulation of carefully designed
differential equations. Notably, flow matching and diffusion models rely on the simulation
of ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and stochastic differential equations (SDEs), re-
spectively (Lipman et al., 2022, 2024; Holderrieth and Erives, 2025).
As introduced in
Chapter 3, diffusion models gradually corrupt input data with Gaussian noise to yield a
simple prior distribution, then learn a reverse denoising process to generate high-fidelity
realistic samples. In contrast, flow matching builds continuous, invertible flow trajectories
that smoothly map a simple noise distribution to the target data distribution.
A key distinction between the two frameworks lies in their mapping properties: flow
models enable exact invertible transformations, whereas the forward diffusion process is
irreversible. Despite this difference, both paradigms generate data through iterative trajec-
tory evolution and eliminate the need for explicit latent posterior inference.
In this section, we first provide formal definitions of ODEs, alongside a discussion of
their numerical simulation. We then introduce deep neural network-based parameterization
strategies for ODEs, which lay the foundation for formulating flow matching models as well
as their core sampling algorithms. Model training methodologies will be comprehensively
explored in subsequent sections.
4.2.1 Flow Models
We begin by revisiting the fundamental concept of ordinary differential equations (ODEs)
(Chen et al., 2018). An ODE solution is characterized by a trajectory, visualized in Fig-
ure 4.5, which corresponds to a function formulated as
x : [0, 1] →RD,
t 7→xt.
This function maps each time instance t ∈[0, 1] to a spatial coordinate in the D-dimensional
Euclidean space RD. For notational clarity, we adopt a consistent font convention through-
out this work: the regular-font variable xt represents the collection of all possible spatial
positions that a particle can attain at time t, whereas the italicized variable xt denotes
a specific, fixed position occupied by the particle in a single realization of the dynamical
process.
Central to any ODE is a vector field (also referred to as a velocity field) v, defined as
the function
v : RD × [0, 1] →RD,
(z, t) 7→vt(z),


152
4.2. FLOW MATCHING
which assigns a D-dimensional velocity vector vt(z) ∈RD to every spatial location z and
time t, encoding the local spatial velocity at the specified position and timestamp (illustrated
in Figure 4.5).
A valid ODE trajectory is constrained to align with the flow dictated by the vector
field vt and is initialized at the starting position x0. Notably, the initial position x0 and
vector field v uniquely determine the resulting trajectory X. This dynamical process can
be formally defined via the initial-value problem below:
d
dtxt = vt(xt);
(ODE)
(4.17a)

(4.17b)
Equation (4.17a) enforces that the temporal derivative of the trajectory xt matches the
velocity direction and magnitude prescribed by the vector field vt at the corresponding
state. Equation (4.17b) specifies the initial constraint, fixing the particle’s position to x0
at the initial timestep t = 0.

spatial position at each corresponding timestamp.
Furthermore, the flow, denoted by ψ, is defined as the ensemble of all trajectories
induced by a vector field v. Each individual trajectory characterizes the continuous path
of a particle propagating through the state space, following the dynamical rules encoded in
v. Given an initial spatial coordinate x0 and a time instance t, the flow map ψt yields the
particle’s subsequent position xt via the relation xt = ψt(x0). This dynamical evolution
is governed by the ODE presented in Equation (4.17a). Substituting the flow formulation
into the core ODE relation yields the rigorous defining equations for the flow structure, as
summarized below:
ψ : RD × [0, 1] →RD,

From an alternative perspective, a flow model corresponds to a continuous-time Markov
process (CTMP) {xt}0≤t≤1. This process is constructed by applying the flow map ψt to
the initial random state variable x0, formulated as:
xt = ψt(x0),
t ∈[0, 1],
where x0 ∼pbase.
(4.19)
The Markov property of {xt} can be verified through the following derivation. For any time
pair satisfying 0 ≤t < s ≤1, we obtain:
xs = ψs(x0) = ψs(ψ−1
t (ψt(x0))) = ψs|t(xt).
(4.20)
The above derivation holds for two key reasons. First, ψt constitutes a diffeomorphism
(Definition 4.1)—a smooth and bijective coordinate transformation with a smooth inverse,
as validated in Theorem 4.2. Second, substituting the CTMP definition xt = ψt(x0) and
defining the conditional flow mapping ψs|t ≜ψs ◦ψ−1
t
(which also preserves the diffeomor-
phism property) completes the equality. Equation (4.20) demonstrates that future states xs
depend solely on the present state xt, which confirms the Markov property of the process
{xt}. Notably, the state dependence inherent to flow-based Markov processes is entirely
deterministic.
Intuitively, vector fields, ODEs, and flows offer three equivalent perspectives describing
the same underlying dynamical structure: vector fields specify ODEs, and the solution
trajectories of these ODEs collectively form flows. As with any differential equation system,
two critical questions arise regarding the well-posedness of the defined ODE: whether a valid
solution exists and, if existent, whether such a solution is unique. A core mathematical result
answers both questions affirmatively under mild regularity conditions on the velocity field
vt, as formalized in the following theorem.
Theorem 4.2: (Flow existence and uniqueness (Perko, 2013; Lipman et al.,
2024))
If v : RD × [0, 1] →RD is continuously differentiable with bounded derivatives,
then the ODE system in (4.18) admits a unique solution characterized by the flow map ψt.
Under these conditions, ψt is a diffeomorphism for all t ∈[0, 1], meaning ψt is continuously
differentiable and possesses a continuously differentiable inverse ψ−1
t .
This theorem establishes that vector fields satisfying standard smoothness conditions
uniquely induce a valid flow. Importantly, these existence and uniqueness prerequisites are
universally satisfied in practical machine learning settings. Since vt(z) is parameterized
via neural networks in our framework, its derivatives are inherently bounded, fulfilling the
theorem’s requirements. Consequently, Theorem 4.2 guarantees robust mathematical well-
posedness for our formulation: flows always exist and correspond to unique ODE
solutions for all scenarios considered in this work.
Conversely, a continuously differentiable flow ψt uniquely determines its associated vec-
tor field.
For any spatial coordinate z ∈RD, the corresponding velocity field can be
recovered from the flow dynamics via the flow ODE (4.18b):
d
dtψt(ez) = vt(ψt(ez)). Lever-
aging the diffeomorphic property of ψt—which ensures invertibility for all t ∈[0, 1]—we
substitute ez = ψ−1
t (z) to invert the flow mapping. This yields the explicit expression for
the unique vector field vt that generates the flow ψt:
vt(z) = ˙ψt(ψ−1

Equation (4.22) defines an explicit flow: the flow corresponds to a linear transformation
acting on the initial system state. This linear vector field flow possesses several fundamen-
tal properties, summarized below:
Linearity: v(z1 + z2) = v(z1) + v(z2) and v(αz) = αv(z) for all z1, z2, z, α.
Determined by eigenvalues: The long-term behavior of the system (e.g., stability,
oscillations, exponential growth/decay) is completely determined by the eigenvalues
of A. For example:
If all eigenvalues have negative real parts, the system is globally asymptotically
stable (trajectories converge to the origin). Specifically, when A = a < 0 is a
negative scalar, all trajectories decay exponentially to the origin z = 0 as time
t increases.
If any eigenvalue has a positive real part, the system is unstable.
□
▶Simulating an ODE.
In most practical scenarios, the flow map ψt cannot be de-
rived in closed form when the velocity field vt deviates from the simple cases presented
earlier. For such general settings, numerical methods are adopted to perform ODE sim-
ulation. Fortunately, numerical ODE solving is a well-established and extensively studied
area in numerical analysis, offering a rich collection of robust and efficient algorithms for
this purpose (Iserles, 2009; Lipman et al., 2024; Holderrieth and Erives, 2025). The Euler
method represents one of the most fundamental, intuitive numerical approaches for ODE
integration, and it is widely used in numerical softwares (Virtanen et al., 2020). When ap-
plied to (6.26). The scheme initializes the particle state as x0 = x0 and iteratively updates


155
CHAPTER 4. FLOW-BASED MODELS
the state via
xt+τ = xt + τ · vt(xt),
t = 0, τ, 2τ, . . . , 1 −τ,
(4.23)
where τ = n−1 > 0 denotes the step size and n ∈N corresponds to the total number of
discrete simulation steps. The Euler update is equivalent to a first-order Taylor expansion
of xt, which yields:
xt+τ = xt + τ d
dtxt + o(τ) = xt + τvt(xt) + o(τ),
(4.24)
where the small-oh function o(·) : R+ →R is a one-dimensional function satisfying o(µ)
µ
→0
as µ →0+. This formulation implies that the Euler method incurs a local truncation error
of o(τ) per integration step, leading to a global accumulated error of o(1) after n = 1/τ
total steps. As a result, the overall approximation error of the Euler method converges to
zero as the step size τ is reduced toward zero.
The Euler method is only one of many available ODE solvers, with higher-order alter-
natives offering substantially improved accuracy in practical applications. A widely used
second-order alternative is the second-order midpoint method. As a second-order numeri-
cal scheme, its global error scales quadratically with step size, delivering far more precise
approximations than the Euler method for identical step sizes. Unlike the Euler method,
which evaluates the derivative solely at the start of each time interval, the midpoint method
approximates the average slope over the interval by computing the derivative at the inter-
val midpoint, yielding a superior representation of the solution curve’s local curvature. The
method follows a two-stage predictor-corrector procedure, summarized as:
xmid = xt + τ
2vt(xt);
(predictor step)
xt+τ = xt + τvt+τ/2(xmid).
(corrector step)
The predictor step performs a “half-step” Euler update to estimate the system state at the
temporal midpoint of the interval. The subsequent corrector step evaluates the velocity
field at this predicted midpoint state and uses this refined slope to compute the full-step
state update from the original time-t state xt. By leveraging midpoint gradient information,
the method effectively mitigates the linearization error inherent in Euler-style straight-line
approximations of curved solution trajectories.
Heun’s method, also known as the improved Euler method or the explicit trapezoidal rule,
is another popular second-order ODE solver. Rather than relying exclusively on either the
initial or midpoint interval slope, Heun’s method constructs a slope estimate by averaging
the derivative evaluated at the start of the interval and the derivative evaluated at an
Euler-predicted end state. Its update rules are defined as:
ext+τ = xt + τvt(xt);
(initial guess of new state)
xt+τ = xt + τ
2(vt(xt) + vt+τ(ext+τ)).
(update with average v)
Conceptually, Heun’s method first generates a preliminary Euler-based prediction of the
next system state, then corrects this tentative update using averaged gradient information
across the time interval. Similar to the midpoint method, it achieves second-order accuracy
and requires two evaluations of the velocity field per time step.


156
4.2. FLOW MATCHING
Runge–Kutta (RK) methods generalize the Euler method by using a weighted average of
intermediate slopes. The first-order forward Euler method uses only the slope at the initial
point, yielding a per-step local error of O(τ 2) and first-order global accuracy. An s-stage
RK method samples s intermediate points within the step size τ, computes the derivative
at each point, and combines these derivatives using optimized weights to achieve up to
s-th order global accuracy.
For example, the second-order Runge–Kutta method (RK2)
introduces an intermediate slope at the half-step point, requiring two function evaluations
per step:
v1 = vt(xt);
v2 = vt+τ/2(xt + τ
2v1);
xt+τ = xt + τ · v2.
The widely used fourth-order Runge–Kutta method (RK4) is defined as follows:
v1 = vt(xt);
v2 = vt+τ/2(xt + τ
2v1);
v3 = vt+τ/2(xt + τ
2v2);
v4 = vt+τ(xt + τv3);
xt+τ = xt + τ
6(v1 + 2v2 + 2v3 + v4).
The weights (1, 2, 2, 1)/6 are derived by matching Taylor expansions, ensuring the first four
order terms are exact.
RK4 requires four function evaluations per step—corresponding
to four neural network forward passes—and strikes a strong balance between accuracy and
computational cost, which explains its widespread use. In general, an s-th order RK method
has the property that halving the step size τ reduces the global numerical error by a factor
of 1/2s. For RK4 in particular, halving the step size reduces the error to 1/16 of its original
value, resulting in a substantial improvement in accuracy.
▶Generative flow models.
Building on the ODE simulation framework introduced
above, we can construct a generative model by parameterizing the vector field via a deep
neural network with trainable parameters θ, denoted as vθ
t . For the purpose of this discus-
sion, vθ

rameter set θ; detailed neural network architecture designs will be elaborated in Chapter 7.
3 The core objective of our generative framework is to produce random samples x ∼pdata
that follow the target data distribution pdata. Notably, ODE systems are inherently de-
terministic and lack stochasticity, which contradicts the random nature of data sampling.
To resolve this discrepancy, stochasticity is introduced by randomizing the ODE’s initial
condition x0. Specifically, we define a base (initial) distribution pbase for the initial state.
3. We adopt a consistent notational convention throughout this work: x0 and x0 correspond to the random
variable and individual sample of the base distribution, while x1 and x1 denote the random variable
and sample of the target data distribution. For notational flexibility, we occasionally use z to represent
samples from the base or intermediate distributions, and reserve x exclusively for samples drawn from
the data distribution.

CHAPTER 4. FLOW-BASED MODELS
In standard implementations, pbase is set to a standard Gaussian distribution N(0, ID) for

time, regardless of the specific distribution chosen. With these definitions, the generative
flow model is fully characterized by the following system of ODE equations:

▶Sampling backwards.
Recall the forward flow ODE, which characterizes the contin-
uous trajectory of the flow map from t = 0 to t = 1:
d
dtψt(x0) = vt(ψt(x0)),
ψ0(x0) = x0.
Forward simulation integrates the ODE from t = 0 to t = 1 to obtain the final state at t = 1.
However, likelihood evaluation for observed data samples requires computing the reverse
flow path (see Section 4.2.4). Given a data point x1 = ψ1(x0) sampled from the target
distribution pdata, we aim to recover its corresponding initial latent state x0 = ψ0(x1) and
calculate the required integral term. This procedure corresponds to backward-time ODE
simulation: we initialize the system at t = 1 with a data sample x1 ∼pdata and integrate
from t = 1 to t = 0 to recover the initial latent state x0. The reverse-time ODE can be
derived through a straightforward time substitution. We define the reversed time variable
s = 1 −t, which gives the differential relation dt = −ds. Substituting this transformation
into the forward ODE yields the following derivation:
d
dtψt(x0) = vt(ψt(x0)) =⇒−d
dsψ1−s(x1) = v1−s(ψ1−s(x1))
=⇒
d
dsψ1−s(x1) = −v1−s(ψ1−s(x1)).
This derivation indicates that backward flow simulation is equivalent to solving the original
ODE system with an inverted time direction and a negated vector field. The complete
workflow for reverse-path sampling is summarized in Algorithm 12.

4.2.2 Conditional and Marginal Probability Path
In the previous subsection, we introduce generative flow models as generative frameworks
parameterized by a neural network vector field vθ
t , where θ ∈RP denotes the set of learnable
model parameters. The training objective of flow models is to optimize the vector field such
that the terminal state x1 follows the target data distribution pdata. This core objective is
formally defined as:
x0 ∼pbase,
dxt = vθ
t (xt) dt
=⇒
x1 ∼pdata.
(4.26)
Through this training paradigm, the learned generative model is able to produce high-
quality, realistic outputs, such as visually coherent images and realistic videos. This learning
formulation motivates the development of flow matching (Lipman et al., 2022, 2024), a
simple, scalable, and state-of-the-art algorithm for training neural vector fields vθ
t .
The foundational step of flow matching is to define a probability path, namely a time-
dependent sequence of probability distributions {pt}. Intuitively, a probability path con-
structs a smooth, gradual interpolation between the base noise distribution pbase and the
target data distribution pdata (see Figure 4.6). This formulation raises a natural question
regarding intermediate timesteps: while our ODE trajectory is strictly constrained by the
boundary conditions x0 ∼pbase at t = 0 and x1 ∼pdata at t = 1, the distribution behavior
for 0 < t < 1 is flexible and unconstrained. A probability path mathematically formalizes
this intermediate distribution evolution.


159
CHAPTER 4. FLOW-BASED MODELS
Given a data sample x ∈RD drawn from pdata, a conditional (interpolating) probability
path is a family of distributions pt|1(· | x) over RD defined for all x ∈RD, satisfying the
following boundary conditions:
p0|1(· | x) = pbase,
p1|1(· | x) = δx,
(conditional boundary conditions)
(4.27)
where δy ≜δ(x −y) denotes the Dirac delta distribution centered at y (i.e., sampling from
δy always returns y). The Dirac delta function δ(y) can be informally interpreted as an
infinitely tall “spike” at y = 0 with the properties
δ(y) = 0,
for y̸ = 0;
Z ∞
−∞
δ(x) dx = 1
=⇒
Z ∞
−∞
f(x)δ(x −y) dx = f(y).
(4.28)
In essence, each conditional probability path continuously transforms the initial base noise
distribution pbase into a discrete, single data point x over the time horizon t ∈[0, 1].
Conceptually, this path can be interpreted as a continuous trajectory traversing the space
of probability distributions.
Each conditional probability path pt|1(z | x) induces a corresponding marginal proba-
bility path pt(z). This marginal distribution is constructed by first sampling a data point
x ∼pdata ≡p1 from the target data distribution, then sampling intermediate states from the
conditional path pt|1(· | x). The formal definition and sampling procedure are summarized
below:
x ∼pdata,
z ∼pt|1(· | x)
=⇒z ∼pt,
(4.29a)
where pt(z) =
Z
pt|1(z | x)pdata(x) dx;
(4.29b)
Notably, while efficient sampling from the marginal distribution pt is feasible via the two-
stage sampling process in (4.29a), explicitly computing the density value pt(z) is intractable
due to the high-dimensional integral in (4.29b). Owing to the boundary properties of the
conditional probability path pt|1(· | x) in (4.27), the resulting marginal path pt achieves
valid distribution interpolation. As t progresses from 0 to 1, pt transitions smoothly from
the base noise distribution pbase to the target data distribution pdata, with all intermediate
distributions serving as continuous blends of the two boundary distributions.
Note 4.4 (Gaussian conditional probability path).
Among existing probability
path formulations, the Gaussian probability path stands out as the most widely adopted
option, serving as the standard probability path for modern state-of-the-art models. We
define two continuously differentiable, monotonic interpolation coefficients αt, βt, referred
to as noise schedulers, that satisfy the boundary conditions α0 = β1 = 0 and α1 = β0 = 1.
A simple and common instantiation is the linear scheduling setup αt = t and βt = 1 −t.
Using these coefficients, we construct the Gaussian conditional probability path as:
pt|1(· | x) = N(αtx, β2
t ID).
(4.30)


160
4.2. FLOW MATCHING
By the defined boundary properties of αt and βt, this formulation naturally satisfies the
conditional path constraints introduced previously:
p0|1(· | x) = N(α0x, β2
0ID) = N(0, ID)
and
p1|1(· | x) = N(α1x, β2
1ID) = δx.
This holds because a Gaussian distribution with zero variance centered at x converges to
the Dirac delta distribution δx. Accordingly, this Gaussian-based conditional path fully
complies with the boundary conditions in (4.27) under the standard base distribution
pbase = N(0, ID), confirming its validity as a legitimate conditional probability path.
Visual examples of this Gaussian interpolation process on image data are presented
in Figure 4.6. The sampling procedure for the corresponding marginal probability path
pt can be explicitly simplified to a closed-form interpolation operation: given a data
sample x ∼pdata and a Gaussian noise sample ϵ ∼N(0, ID), the intermediate state
z ∼pt is computed as:
x ∼pdata, ϵ ∼pbase = N(0, ID)
=⇒
z = αtx + βtϵ ∼pt.
(4.31)
Intuitively, this interpolation mechanism adjusts the noise magnitude adaptively over
time. Lower values of t introduce larger amounts of Gaussian noise into the data sample,
gradually corrupting the original data. At t = 0, the data component vanishes com-
pletely, leaving only pure Gaussian noise, which aligns with the definition of the base
distribution.
4.2.3 Conditional and Marginal Vector Fields
A probability path {pt}0≤t≤1 defines the distributions xt ∼pt that the points xt along a
trajectory are intended to follow. The key question is then: how can we construct a vector
field under which trajectories xt adhere to this probability path? Flow matching directly
provides such a vector field—known as the marginal vector field—which we introduce in
this subsection.

methodology adopted throughout this book.

We emphasize the conceptual distinction between the marginal dynamics (4.36) and the
conditional dynamics (4.32).
As stated by Bayes’ rule (1.10), the quantity
p1|t(x | z) = pt|1(z | x)pdata(x)
pt(z)
= “posterior over data samples x given noisy data z”,
defines the posterior distribution over clean data samples x, where pdata(x) acts as the
prior distribution. From this perspective, the marginal vector field is essentially a weighted
average. For each candidate data point x, we take the conditional dynamics vθ∗
t (z | x)—
the vector field direction that drives trajectories toward x—and weight it by the posterior
probability that the noisy state z originates from the clean data point x.
Intuitively,
Theorem 4.5 states that averaging these conditional vector fields over all possible data
points yields the valid marginal vector field for the full probability path.
We can alternatively interpret this theorem using the conditional expectation formula
in (1.26). Consider the random pair (z, x) with joint density pt,1(z, x) = pt|1(z | x)pdata(x),
so that z ∼pt|1(· | x). Applying the conditional expectation formula rewrites the marginal
vector field definition (4.35) as

The continuity equation can be viewed as a special case of the Fokker–Planck equation, as
further detailed in Lemma 5.7.
To build intuition, we briefly interpret the continuity equation.
The left-hand side
∂tpt(z) quantifies the instantaneous temporal change in probability density at the state z.
Intuitively, this change corresponds to the net influx of probability mass at that location.
In flow-based generative models, particle trajectories xt evolve according to the vector field
vθ∗
t . From physical intuition, divergence characterizes the net outward flux of a vector field.
Consequently, the negative divergence encodes the net inward flux. When weighted by the
local probability density pt(z), the term −div(ptvθ∗
t ) exactly describes the net inflow of
probability mass at z. Since the total probability mass remains normalized at all times,
these two quantities must be equal, which validates the continuity equation.
We now present the formal proof of the marginalization result stated in Theorem 4.5.

4.2.4 Tractable Likelihood Computation via Hutchinson’s Estimator
A key advantage of flow-based generative models is their ability to compute exact log-
likelihoods ln p1(x) tractably for all x ∈RD.
This property follows from a continuity
relation termed the instantaneous change of variables (Chen et al., 2018):

with respect to time and substitute the continuity equation (4.39) alongside the flow ODE
(4.18b). Integrating both sides of (4.43) from t = 0 to t = 1 and rearranging terms yields:
ln p1(ψ1(z)) = ln p0(ψ0(z)) −

▶Hutchinson’s trace estimator.

Unlike the original divergence computation in div(vt)(ψt(z)) in (4.43) or (4.44), for a specific

In practical implementations, M = 1 is commonly adopted, where a single noise sample is
resampled for each data point. Although this introduces stochastic noise into the likelihood
estimate, the impact is negligible in practice. This is because the overall training objective is
optimized via stochastic gradient descent, which inherently tolerates noisy gradients. Crit-
ically, the estimator remains strictly unbiased, meaning its expected value exactly matches
the true trace and log-likelihood.
▶Reverse-mode automatic differentiation.
As discussed above, for a fixed noise

of a vector field f(z) ≜vt(z) : RD →RD. To do this, we first need to compute the vector
ϵ⊤Jϵ. Naively, computing the full Jacobian matrix J takes D passes of backpropagation.
However, we can compute the product ϵ⊤Jϵ in a single pass.

4. Note that depending on the layout convention (numerator vs. denominator layout), this might appear

In practice, deep learning frameworks (like PyTorch or TensorFlow) implement this effi-

The trick is known as reverse-mode automatic differentiation (backpropagation) and can be
concluded in four steps:
(i) Forward pass. Compute the output of the vector field f(z) for the input coordinate
z.
(ii) Scalar projection. Calculate the scalar objective L = ϵ⊤f(z). This is a simple dot
product (or sum of elementwise multiplication).
(iii) Reverse pass (backpropagation). Evaluate the gradient of the scalar objective L with
respect to the input z.

By definition of the gradient, g = J⊤ϵ.
(iv) Final dot product. Compute the scalar trace estimate via g⊤ϵ.
For the complexity, we can conclude that
Naive approach. Computing the full Jacobian J requires D backward passes (com-
puting ∂fi/∂z for each i). This results in a quadratic computational cost of O(D) ×
O(D) = O(D2).
Reverse-mode automatic differentiation. We treat L = ϵ⊤f(z) as a single scalar loss.
Computing the gradient of a scalar loss with respect to inputs requires exactly one
backward pass, yielding a linear computational cost of O(D).
This optimization allows the Hutchinson trace estimator to be evaluated in linear time with
respect to input dimensionality D, rather than the prohibitive quadratic time complexity of
naive Jacobian-based computation, making high-dimensional flow model training feasible.
▶Obtaining the log-likelihood.
As established, naive explicit Jacobian trace computa-
tion incurs prohibitive O(D2) complexity. By combining Hutchinson’s trace estimator with
reverse-mode automatic differentiation, we reduce this computational overhead to O(D),
enabling efficient log-likelihood estimation for high-dimensional inputs. In summary, un-
biased estimation of the data log-likelihood ln p1(x) relies on parallel backward simulation
of two coupled flow ODEs (implemented via the Euler method in Algorithm 12). The two
evolving components are defined as follows:

4.2.5 Learning the Marginal Vector Field
We now proceed to formalize the training algorithm. The core objective of flow matching is
to train a neural network vθ
t —parameterized by learnable weights θ ∈RP —to approximate

4.2. FLOW MATCHING
the target marginal vector field vθ∗
t . Per Theorem 4.5, exact alignment between vθ
t and
vθ∗
t
guarantees that the terminal samples x1 ∼pdata follow the target data distribution.
A natural and intuitive strategy to achieve vθ
t ≈vθ∗
t
is to minimize a mean squared error
objective 5, termed the flow matching (FM) loss, which is defined as:
JFM(θ) ≜Et∼Unif,z∼pt
vθ
t (z) −vθ∗

pt|1(z | x)pdata(x) dx denotes the marginal probability path, and the final
equality derives from the sampling procedure outlined in (4.29). Here, “Unif” denotes a
uniform distribution over the interval [0, 1]. Conceptually, this loss operates via the following
sampling pipeline: first, sample a continuous time variable t ∈[0, 1] uniformly at random.
Second, draw a data sample x from the empirical data distribution pdata, then sample a
latent point z from the conditional path distribution pt|1(· | x) (e.g., via noise injection) and
evaluate the network output vθ
t (z). Finally, compute the squared Euclidean discrepancy
between the network prediction and the target marginal vector field vθ∗
t (z).
However, this formulation presents a critical limitation. While Theorem 4.5 provides
an exact analytical expression for the marginal vector field vθ∗
t , the associated integral is
computationally intractable, precluding direct optimization of the standard flow matching
loss. To resolve this issue, we leverage the tractability of the conditional vector field vθ∗

This objective differs from the original flow matching loss in (4.55) by replacing the in-

Since closed-form expressions exist for vθ∗

A natural question arises:
why optimize against the conditional vector field when
the ultimate goal is to match the marginal vector field?
Critically, explicit regression
on the tractable conditional vector field implicitly induces regression on the intractable
marginal vector field. The following theoretical result rigorously validates this key intu-
ition.
Theorem 4.8: (FM and CFM losses (Holderrieth and Erives, 2025))
The marginal
FM loss and CFM loss differ only by a parameter-independent constant. Their relationship
can be formulated as:
JFM(θ) = JCFM(θ) + C,
where the constant C is independent of the learnable network parameters θ. As a direct
consequence, the two loss functions yield identical parameter gradients:
∇θJFM(θ) = ∇θJCFM(θ).
Accordingly, stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and other standard optimization proce-
dures minimize JCFM(θ) in a manner fully equivalent to optimizing the original intractable
JFM(θ).
5. An alternative loss function is discussed in Problems 4.4–4.5.

The above equivalence theorem implies that, given a sufficiently expressive neural net-
work parameterization, the minimizer bθ of the CFM loss JCFM(θ) exactly recovers the
target marginal vector field, such that vbθ
t (z) = vθ∗
t (z). This validates the core training
paradigm of flow matching, which optimizes the tractable conditional flow matching loss
as a viable surrogate for the original intractable marginal flow matching objective. The
complete flow matching training pipeline is summarized in Algorithm 14 (e.g, for Gaussian
conditional optimal transport (CondOT) probability paths; see Note 4.9).
This algorithm exhibits three notable and advantageous properties. First, the training
process is entirely simulation-free: it eliminates the need to solve or roll out ODE trajecto-
ries during optimization, which substantially reduces computational overhead compared to
traditional flow-based generative models. Second, the training objective reduces to a simple
regression task, where the network is trained to fit the analytically known conditional vec-
tor field vθ∗
t (z | x), making the optimization framework analogous to standard supervised
learning. Third, the formulation is structurally concise with minimal computational com-
plexity, rendering it highly practical for large-scale machine learning systems. These merits

We introduce the score-based interpretation of DDPMs in Section 3.2.6. This
relationship can also be characterized from a reverse process perspective (Song
and Ermon, 2019; Bishop and Bishop, 2023). To elaborate on why optimizing
a score function yields valid generative modeling, we revisit energy-based models for foun-
dational context LeCun et al. (2006). Arbitrarily flexible probability distributions can be
formulated in the standard energy-based form:
p(x) = 1
Cϕ
exp(−fϕ(x)),
(5.1)
where fϕ(x) denotes a flexible, parameterizable energy function, typically instantiated as
a neural network, and Cϕ is the normalizing constant that enforces the valid probability
density condition
R
p(x) dx = 1. Maximum likelihood estimation is a conventional approach
to learn such distributions.
However, this method requires explicit computation of the
intractable normalizing constant Cϕ =
R
exp(−fϕ(x)) dx, which is infeasible for highly
complex energy functions fϕ(x).
A canonical strategy to bypass explicit normalization constant computation is to train
a neural network sθ(x) to approximate the score function ∇ln p(x) ≡∇x ln p(x) of the
target distribution p(x). This approach is motivated by differentiating the log-transformed
form of Equation (5.1), which yields the following exact equivalence and neural network
approximation:
∇x ln p(x) = ∇x ln
 1
Cϕ
exp(−fϕ(x))

= −∇xfϕ(x) ≃sθ(x).
(5.2)
Notably, the resulting score approximation is fully normalization-free and can be flexibly
parameterized by neural networks.
Score matching frameworks are formalized via a loss function that trains the model
score sθ(x) to align with the true data score ∇x ln p(x) of the underlying data-generating
distribution p(x) ≜pdata(x). The standard training objective is the expected squared error
between the predicted and ground-truth score functions, known as the Fisher divergence
(FD) between the model and data distributions:
J (θ) = Ep(x)
sθ(x) −∇x ln p(x)

2
2

=
Z
p(x)
sθ(x) −∇x ln p(x)

2
2 dx.
(5.3)
Fisher divergence directly measures discrepancies in the score vectors that constitute Fisher
information. Minimizing FD aligns the local Fisher curvature of the learned model distri-
bution with the intrinsic information geometry of the true data distribution. Unlike KL
divergence, which operates on raw probability density values, FD relies exclusively on log-
density gradients (scores). This key property eliminates the need to compute intractable
normalization constants Cϕ, making FD particularly suitable for training diffusion genera-
tive models.
The score function admits an intuitive geometric interpretation: for each data point x,
the gradient of the log-likelihood with respect to x indicates the direction in data space
along which the likelihood of the sample increases 1. Collectively, the score function defines a
continuous vector field over the entire data space, pointing toward the high-density modes of
1. See, for example, Lu (2025).


176
5.1. SCORE MATCHING
the data distribution. This geometric structure is visualized in the right panel of Figure 5.2.
By accurately learning the score field of the true data distribution, we can generate novel
samples via iterative optimization: starting from arbitrary initial points in the ambient
data space, we iteratively follow the score vector field to converge toward data modes. This
generative sampling paradigm is termed Langevin dynamics, defined mathematically as:
xt+1 ←xt + ζ
2∇x ln p(xt) +
p
ζ ϵ,
t = 0, 1, . . . , T,
(5.4)
where the initial sample x0 is randomly sampled from a pre-specified prior distribution (e.g.,
a uniform distribution), and ϵ ∼N(0, ID) denotes Gaussian noise. The injected stochastic
noise prevents generated samples from collapsing deterministically to distribution modes,
thereby preserving sample diversity, as discussed in Section 3.2.2. The evolution of sam-
ple particles under Langevin dynamics is illustrated in Figure 5.1. Furthermore, since the
learned score function is deterministic, the additive noise term introduces essential stochas-
ticity to the generative process and avoids rigid deterministic sampling trajectories. This
stochasticity is especially beneficial when sampling initial points located between multiple
data modes. A comprehensive visual illustration of Langevin dynamics sampling and the
regularization benefits of noise injection is presented in Figure 5.2.

5.1. Score Matching
However, the loss function defined in (5.3) suffers from a critical practical limitation: neither
the original loss nor its direct transformations can be optimized efficiently, as the formulation
is computationally intractable. To address this issue, we introduce two practical variants
of the score-matching loss: denoising score matching loss and sliced score matching loss.
5.1.1 Denoising Score Matching
A fundamental limitation of the loss function in (5.3) is that it cannot be minimized
directly, since the ground-truth data score ∇x ln pdata(x) of the true data distribution
is analytically unknown.
In practice, we only have access to a finite empirical dataset
X = {x1, x2, . . . , xN} ∼pdata(x), from which we construct the empirical data distribution:
pX (x) = 1
N
N
X
n=1
δxn(x),
(5.5)

CHAPTER 5. SCORE MATCHING AND STOCHASTIC DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS
Figure 5.2: Presents four distinct sampling trajectories generated by Langevin dynamics
for a Gaussian mixture model, all initialized from a starting point between two modes.
The left subfigure visualizes these trajectories over a three-dimensional contour landscape
of the target distribution, whereas the right subfigure overlays the trajectories against the
ground-truth score function field. Owing to the stochastic noise term embedded within
the Langevin sampling update rule, samples originating from a single fixed initialization
can converge to different distribution modes across independent trials. In the absence of
this noise component, sampling initiated at a fixed point would follow a fully deterministic
trajectory and invariably converge to the identical mode in every run.
where δxn = δ(x −xn) is the Dirac delta distribution (see (4.28)) centered at xn (i.e.,
sampling from δxn always returns xn).
The empirical Dirac-based distribution in (5.5) is non-differentiable with respect to x,
making its corresponding score function unavailable for direct computation. To overcome
this obstacle, we smooth the discrete data points via a noise injection strategy, yielding a
continuous, differentiable density estimate. This kernel-based smoothing technique corre-
sponds to a Parzen estimator, or kernel density estimator, formulated as:

Remark 5.1 (Equivalence to DDPMs).
By adopting the diffusion transition kernel
from (3.10), we derive the diffusion-specific score function:
∇zt ln q(zt | x0) =

1

ϵt,

This result demonstrates that the empirical score-matching loss in (5.8c) essentially min-
imizes the prediction error between the network output and the Gaussian noise injected
during the diffusion forward process. As a consequence, this loss shares an identical opti-
mal solution with the standard noise-prediction loss used in DDPMs, as shown in (3.51b)
and the score-based DDPM formulation (3.65). The score network sθ(z) is functionally
equivalent to the DDPM noise-prediction network ϵθ(z), differing only by a constant
scaling factor −1/√1 −αt (Song and Ermon, 2019). This optimization objective de-
fines denoising score matching, which establishes a rigorous theoretical bridge between
score-based generative models and denoising diffusion probabilistic models.
5.1.2 Sliced Score Matching
As discussed previously, direct optimization of Fisher divergence is infeasible, as it requires
evaluating the intractable score function of the unknown true data distribution. Rather
than performing explicit denoising on observed data, we circumvent the need to explicitly
compute the underlying data distribution pdata(x) by directly training a score network sθ(x)
to approximate the data score ∇x ln pdata(x). This paradigm is grounded in the following
theoretical result:

Despite eliminating the intractable ground-truth score term, the loss function J ′′(θ)

5.1.3 Annealed Langevin Dynamics
We have introduced the procedure for learning the score function from training datasets and
generating new samples from the learned distribution via Langevin sampling. Nevertheless,
three key limitations exist for this standard framework (Song and Ermon, 2019).
▶Manifold hypothesis.
First, we elaborate on the manifold hypothesis, which states
that real-world data typically resides on a low-dimensional manifold embedded within a
high-dimensional ambient space.
This indicates that raw or preprocessed data contains
substantial redundant information across most dimensions, such that only a small subset
of dimensions is required for complete data representation. Consequently, data occupies
only a constrained subspace (a low-dimensional manifold) rather than uniformly filling the
entire high-dimensional encoding space. This inherent property leads to non-convergent
loss optimization in the training of score-based generative models. Specifically, the score
function ∇x ln p(x) is mathematically defined over the entire encoding space. However, for
manifold-restricted data distributions, the score function is ill-defined in all regions outside
the data-supporting manifold.
▶Inaccurate score estimation.
The score matching objectives defined in (5.3) and
(5.11) yield consistent score estimators only if the data distribution has full support over
the entire ambient space. Consistency breaks down when data lies on a low-dimensional
manifold. Score estimation accuracy further degrades in low-data-density regions, due to
density-based loss weighting and insufficient training samples covering these distribution
regions.
Such inaccurate score estimates corrupt the sampling trajectories of Langevin
dynamics (5.4). Since Langevin sampling initializes from random high-dimensional noise


181
CHAPTER 5. SCORE MATCHING AND STOCHASTIC DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS
and evolves strictly following the learned score function, noisy or biased score estimates
produce suboptimal generated samples and require substantially more iteration steps to
achieve convergence.
▶Large deviation between generated distribution and reality.
Inaccurate score
estimation inevitably degrades generation quality, resulting in generated samples that fail to
align with the true underlying data distribution. Even with perfectly accurate score function
estimation, standard Langevin sampling may still produce biased samples for data distribu-
tions composed of disjoint mixture components. Consider a mixture data distribution for-
mulated as pdata(x) = πp1(x)+(1−π)p2(x), where p1(x) and p2(x) denote normalized distri-
butions with disjoint supports, and π ∈(0, 1) denotes the mixing coefficient. Within the sup-
port of p1(x), the score satisfies ∇x ln pdata(x) = ∇x (ln π + ln p1(x)) = ∇x ln p1(x). Simi-
larly, within the support of p2(x), ∇x ln pdata(x) = ∇x (ln(1 −π) + ln p2(x)) = ∇x ln p2(x).
Notably, the score function of the mixture distribution is independent of the mixing
coefficient π. As Langevin dynamics generate samples solely guided by ∇x ln pdata(x), the
resulting samples fail to reflect the true mixing weights.
This conclusion also holds for
practical scenarios with approximately disjoint modes, where distinct modes are separated
by low-density transitional regions. Although Langevin sampling is theoretically valid in
such cases, it requires extremely small step sizes and vast iteration counts to achieve mode
mixing.
This phenomenon is visualized in Figure 5.2, where the ground-truth score function
is invariant to the varying weights of the three Gaussian mixture components. From the
illustrated initialization point, standard Langevin dynamics yield nearly equal sampling
probability for all modes, even when the rightmost mode possesses a significantly higher
weight in the true Gaussian mixture model.
▶Annealed Langevin dynamics.
All aforementioned issues can be mitigated by in-
troducing Gaussian noise with sufficiently large variance σ2 via the kernel function in (5.7),
which effectively smooths the original data distribution. First, Gaussian noise spans full
support over the entire ambient space, lifting manifold-constrained data to the complete
high-dimensional space and eliminating undefined score regions. Second, large-magnitude
Gaussian noise expands the effective support of each data mode, enriching training signals
in low-density regions. Third, hierarchical noise injection with progressively increasing vari-
ance yields intermediate smoothed distributions that faithfully preserve the ground-truth
mixture mixing coefficients.
Nevertheless, excessively large noise variance introduces severe distortion to the original
data distribution, inducing new biases in score function modeling. To resolve this trade-
off, we adopt a decreasing sequence of noise variances σ2
1 > σ2
2 > . . . > σ2
L. The smallest
variance σ2
L is set sufficiently small to retain accurate original distribution characteristics,
while the largest variance σ2
1 is large enough to eliminate the aforementioned manifold and
estimation artifacts.
The score network is accordingly modified to take the noise variance as an auxiliary
input, denoted sθ(x, σ). The network is trained via a weighted combination of standard
score matching losses (5.3), where each loss term corresponds to a specific noise level and
its perturbed data distribution.
For a given data sample xn, the training objective is
formulated as:

(5.14)
where λ(i) denotes positive weight coefficients conditioned on the noise level i. This training
paradigm is highly analogous to the optimization strategy for hierarchical denoising net-
works, and the objective function closely matches the final loss formulation for variational
diffusion models in (3.65).
After training, sample generation proceeds by sequentially executing Langevin sampling
steps across all noise levels i = 1, 2, . . . , L. This paradigm is defined as annealed Langevin
dynamics, which shares core design principles with the sampling algorithm for denoising
diffusion models (Algorithm 7). The full pseudocode of annealed Langevin dynamics is
provided in Algorithm 15. Sampling initialization is sampled from a fixed prior distribution
(e.g., uniform distribution), and the sampling trajectory of each noise level inherits the
final samples from the previous level. By progressively reducing noise levels and sampling
step sizes, the generated samples gradually refine and converge to valid data modes. This
sampling mechanism is consistent with the iterative refinement process in the Markovian
HVAE interpretation of variational diffusion models, where randomly initialized data vectors
are optimized iteratively under decreasing noise perturbations.
